# Elite-bar review — findings register (2026-09-04)

Founder directive, verbatim (2026-09-03):

> I want you to look at this system from the lenses of a 50+ person elite
> development team including c suite individuals and best in class engineers
> and product designers. This app should be held to the highest bar in every
> applicable aspect and look to comparable interfaces to help shape this and
> give this your deepest attention to detail in every aspect.

Twelve independent reviewers, one discipline each, read the repository at
`72abef3e` and reported against their field's best-in-class bar. This file is
the durable register of what they found: it is **raw reviewer output, not
adjudicated truth**. Every entry is a hypothesis until verified against the
code — that is the wave rule, and two of these lenses have already been shown
to overlap and to overstate. Verified items graduate out of this file into a
dated commit or a decision record; refuted items are struck with a reason.

Severity is the reviewer's own. `S` = up to half a day, `M` = 1-3 days, `L` = a week or more.

## Status

- **Closed already:** the six cross-tenant routes (commit `6c8bd244`), which
  three lenses found independently. The systemic cause — 93 repository
  methods taking the tenant key as an *optional* parameter, 111 call sites
  omitting it — is now a down-only ratchet with a zero-tolerance rule for
  handlers that never name an organization.
- **In flight:** the customer-vocabulary program (81 banned-word violations
  on customer surfaces) and the extraction of the founder-only agent console
  out of the `/ai` door.
- **Everything else below is unadjudicated.**


## CEO — positioning, wedge legibility, first-run value, pricing/expansion, and whether a stranger understands the product in ten seconds

**The landing page sells an overnight-autonomy product ("22 leads comped overnight," "11 replies drafted by Pax," "~62 hr week → ~22 hr week") that the founder has explicitly and correctly decided not to build — while the genuinely differentiated thing AcreOS *does* have, a machine that asks before it acts and shows its receipts, appears nowhere above the fold.**


### Already at or above the bar — do not "fix" these

- The refund policy (client/src/pages/terms.tsx:199-262) is genuinely best-in-class: 30 days, no questions, named exclusions for pass-through costs, a stated processing window, and an explicit good-faith clause for renewals. It is a real risk-reversal asset and the reason a price increase is safe to test. Do not touch it.
- Pricing has exactly one source of truth. client/src/lib/pricing-copy.ts derives every price from TIER_PRICES_CENTS and every seat/limit bullet from TIER_LIMITS, so the landing card, the /pricing table, the checkout amount and MRR math cannot drift. The header records the two historical drift episodes this fixed. This is the pattern every other claim on the site should follow.
- The refusal to fabricate social proof. Quotes.tsx replaced a testimonial grid with mechanic statements, and the hero's trust band leads with five named federal data sources instead of invented user counts. With zero customers this is the right call and it reads as confidence, not absence.
- The free, no-signup streaming parcel check (server/routes-public-parcel-check.ts, linked as Hero cta2) is the single best strategic asset on the page — a stranger watching real government data resolve on their own address is a demo no competitor gives away. It is correctly built and correctly labeled; the only mistake is that it is not the center of gravity.
- The witnessed-send posture itself. shared/pax-controls.ts:44 OFFERED_STANCES with STANCE_RULINGS requiring a dated founder document, and a kernel that freezes every send until a human taps, is a defensible category position — "the AI that never sends without you" — that no incumbent can copy without rebuilding their product. It is the answer to the positioning problem, not a constraint on it.
- Honest first-value instrumentation: server/services/onboarding/sampleSeeder.ts emits no lifecycle events, so loading 50 fake leads does not satisfy the TTFV metric defined in shared/schema/onboarding-funnel.ts. The temptation to let sample data score as activation was available and was declined.
- The signature route's refusal (server/routes-doc-system.ts:931-956) — an operator may sign only for themselves, with a written explanation of why attesting a counterparty's signature is a responsibility AcreOS cannot discharge. That is exactly the judgment an elite team wants from its own code comments.
- The truth-note discipline in client/src/pages/landing/copy.ts — dated, signed corrections recording what was removed and why, including corrections to earlier corrections. The findings above are almost all failures of coverage, not of intent; the intent is already institutionalized here.

### Benchmarked against

- Linear — the promise and the demo are the same object; the landing claims only the loop the product actually runs, in the tense it runs in. AcreOS's landing claims a nightly loop that does not exist while hiding the loop that does.
- Stripe — precision as a trust device: retention, refund and compliance claims are stated exactly and linked to the governing document, never rounded to 'nothing' or 'always'. Directly applicable to the FAQ retention and SOC 2 lines.
- Ramp — every quantified outcome claim carries a defined measurement behind it. AcreOS's '~62 hr week → ~22 hr week' has none, and the gate that should have caught it cannot see the unit.
- Attio — segments the audience without publishing an internal maturity grade beside each segment; where AcreOS publishes one, it currently contradicts its own hero.
- DealMachine — activation is the user's own data in under a minute (drive a street, get the owner). AcreOS owns the equivalent asset (no-signup parcel check) and does not use it in first-run.
- REsimpli — prices at $149-$599 and runs explicit competitor-comparison campaigns; AcreOS's ceiling is $79 and its two comparison pages are orphaned with zero internal links.
- Carrot — proves that organic content is the viable zero-spend channel in this exact category; AcreOS has 13 content files and a publishing engine switched off.
- Vanta — compliance status published without a self-expiring public target date.

### Findings


#### [critical/M] The landing's core narrative is overnight autonomy; the product is deliberately ask-first and has no overnight engine

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing/DayInLife.tsx:30,32,38; Quotes.tsx:37; copy.ts:168,181; FAQ.tsx:39`
- **Defect:** Four separate landing surfaces describe a nightly autonomous run: DayInLife.tsx:30 "Inbox opens to 14 overnight replies. 11 drafted by Pax", :32 "22 leads comped overnight. 4 surfaced as in-buy-box", :38 "Overnight: comps run, replies drafted"; Quotes.tsx:37 "Comps pulled, scored, and ranked overnight against the operator's buy-box"; copy.ts:168 "Lists pulled, replies drafted, your drips sent — overnight"; copy.ts:181 "Pax monitors the pipeline overnight: pulls comps, scores leads, drafts replies"; FAQ.tsx:39 "the first list pulls overnight." None of it exists. There is no buy-box scan engine and no scheduled customer list job — copy.ts's own truth-note (lines 42-56) says exactly that and retired the "Monday at 6am" sentence for it, then left the same claim standing four lines below in step 2. Reply drafting is operator-initiated: routes-ai-draft.ts:16-18 documents the switch as "Draft a reply when I open a message." The nightly job that does exist (server/jobs/leadCampaignJobs.ts) scores and stages leads and contains no comp logic at all. Meanwhile the real product story — founder ruling 2026-09-02, every Pax-written message waits for a human tap at every stance (shared/pax-controls.ts:44 OFFERED_STANCES) — is the single most defensible claim in this category and is buried in FAQ item 3.
- **Fix:** Rewrite the AFTER column, Quotes card 02, and how.steps[1] to the enforced mechanic: new lead stored → workflow conditions evaluated on ingest → scored → Pax drafts on your tap → nothing sends until you approve, with a receipt. Then promote that to the hero: the wedge sentence should name the control, not just the consolidation. "Every message waits for your tap, and shows you why" is a promise no competitor in this category can make and the code already enforces.
- **Comparable:** Linear and Stripe both sell the *mechanism* they actually shipped, in the verb tense it runs in. Linear never claims the issue tracker triages overnight; it claims a specific keystroke-level loop and shows it. The reason their 10-second test passes is that the promise and the demo are the same object.
- **Evidence:** Read all 15 files in client/src/pages/landing/**; read server/routes-ai-draft.ts:1-45; read server/jobs/leadCampaignJobs.ts:1-60 and grepped it for 'comp' (only match is the word 'decomposing' in the header); listed server/jobs/ (40 files, no comp or buy-box job); grepped server/services|jobs|routes for buy.box/buyBox (only founder-side growthTargets.ts and parcel-check prose). Read docs/autonomous/AUTONOMY_SPEC.md §7 founder question 1 and shared/pax-controls.ts:44.

#### [critical/S] FAQ tells prospects "AcreOS retains nothing after cancellation" — the binding privacy policy on the same site says the opposite, and no customer-triggered deletion path exists

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing/FAQ.tsx:43 vs client/src/pages/privacy.tsx:314-333, 368-372; server/routes-admin.ts:1137-1171`
- **Defect:** FAQ.tsx:43: "No data hostage-taking — AcreOS retains nothing after cancellation." privacy.tsx:314-322 says account and lead data are retained for "Duration of active subscription + 90 days post-cancellation"; :328-333 says note/financial records 7 years and billing records 7 years. Worse, there is no cancellation-triggered purge at all: server/jobs/dataRetention.ts:16-33 is a fixed age-based sweep of operational tables with no org or subscription-status predicate, and the only route that calls deleteOrganization is POST /api/admin/organizations/:id/delete (routes-admin.ts:1137), gated by isFounderAdmin and requiring the founder to type the org name. That route's own success message (routes-admin.ts:1170) says "audit_events retained under GDPR Art. 17(3)(b); financial_ledger detached." A grep for a customer-facing delete-account UI across client/src returns nothing. So the sentence is false three ways: against the policy, against the retention job, and against the erasure route's own output.
- **Fix:** Replace with the true, still-strong answer: "Export everything at any time, including for 30 days after you cancel. We keep account and lead data for 90 days after cancellation, and financial records for 7 years where law requires it — see the retention table." Then build the customer-side erasure request that Section 7 of the privacy policy already promises.
- **Comparable:** Stripe and Ramp both make the retention promise *precisely* ("deleted within N days of a verified request, except X required by law") and link the policy from the marketing claim. Precision reads as more trustworthy than "nothing," not less.
- **Evidence:** Read FAQ.tsx in full; read privacy.tsx:300-372; read server/jobs/dataRetention.ts:1-50; read server/routes-admin.ts:1125-1175; grepped server+client for deleteOrganization callers (only routes-admin.ts) and for a customer 'Delete account' UI (no hits).

#### [critical/M] The positioning band tells a stranger that land — the wedge the hero just claimed depth in — is Beta, while fix-and-flip renders as the most mature vertical on the page

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing/Positioning.tsx (TIERS via deriveLandingTiers) ← shared/business-types/publicClaims.ts:55-89; shared/business-types.ts:136-176, 480-483`
- **Defect:** PUBLIC_CLAIM_DEMOTIONS (publicClaims.ts:77-89) demotes 13 verticals to beta — including land_flipper and note_investor. hybrid is excluded from the chips (Positioning.tsx LANDING_EXCLUDED_IDS), so the band renders 12 Beta chips, 2 solid core chips, and an empty roadmap tier. The two undemoted are fix_and_flip and subdivider. A visitor therefore reads hero eyebrow "For property investors — deepest in land" (copy.ts:69) and, one section later, "Land flipper [Beta]" beside an unqualified "Fix-and-flip." That inversion is not just awkward, it is wrong on the merits: business-types.ts:139-144 records the founder's 2026-07-11 decision demoting fix_and_flip because AcreOS maps it onto the LAND data plane and has no residential comps source, and the product's own onboarding copy warns that customer "residential comps run through your connected ATTOM key — the vertical is in beta" (client/src/lib/onboarding-verticals.ts:105). The landing and the signup wizard say opposite things about the same vertical within three minutes of each other. The root cause is stated plainly in publicClaims.ts:55-60: the demotion criterion is "no production surface records a decision snapshot" — an internal instrumentation fact — and it is being published as a customer-readiness grade.
- **Fix:** Separate the two questions. Keep the internal evidence ladder for the readiness ratchet, and give the public band its own explicit, founder-set tier per vertical (land + notes = core, everything else = beta or roadmap by decision, not by telemetry). If the evidence rule is to stay authoritative, then fix the evidence for land first — it is the wedge — before publishing a grade that undercuts the hero.
- **Comparable:** Attio and Lofty both segment by "who this is for" without publishing an internal maturity grade next to each segment. Where a vendor does publish maturity (Vanta's framework list), the grade is a customer-readiness statement, not an internal telemetry-evidence statement.
- **Evidence:** Read Positioning.tsx in full; read shared/business-types/publicClaims.ts:40-95; grepped shared/business-types.ts for all 15 ids and their maturity lines; read the fix_and_flip block (136-176) and subdivider block (480-505); read client/src/lib/onboarding-verticals.ts:96-110.

#### [high/S] The one number left on the hero is verified against its own marketing comment, and nothing measures it

- **Where:** `scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:236-245; client/src/pages/landing/copy.ts:95; shared/schema/onboarding-funnel.ts:14-22`
- **Defect:** The audit registers "Pax pulls your first county list inside 10 minutes" (hero ctaSub, copy.ts:95; Features.tsx:24) and sources it to "Founder-retained landing targets (truth-note record) / ref: client/src/pages/landing/copy.ts truth-engine notes" — the claim is verified against a comment in the same file that makes the claim. That is precisely the circularity the file's own header condemns at lines 6-13 ("the audit was certifying a fabricated claim against a fabricated source"). And the target is not instrumented anywhere: firstValueInstrumentation defines first value as property.created | deal.created | pax.first_message | onboarding.step_5_completed with a median target of 90 SECONDS (onboarding-funnel.ts:14-22). So the company runs two incompatible time-to-first-value definitions with two different targets, and the public one is the unmeasured one.
- **Fix:** Either instrument "signup → first list imported" as a named lifecycle event and publish the measured median, or retire the number and say what the setup actually is ("Import a county CSV and every record lands scored"). A source paragraph may only cite a measurement or an enforced code path — never a comment.
- **Comparable:** Ramp's public performance numbers each cite a measured internal metric with a definition. The discipline is: the claim's source must be a measurement, never a note explaining why the claim was kept.
- **Evidence:** Read scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:85-215 and 215-345 in full; read shared/schema/onboarding-funnel.ts:1-60; read client/src/lib/onboarding-checklist.ts:163-172 (the step is "Import a CSV of owners from your target county").

#### [high/S] The claims audit's completeness regex cannot see the biggest outcome claim on the page — a 40-hour-per-week savings with zero customers behind it

- **Where:** `scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:214 (NUMBER_CLAIM); client/src/pages/landing/DayInLife.tsx:52,68`
- **Defect:** DayInLife.tsx:52 prints "~62 hr week" over the Before column and :68 "~22 hr week" over the After column — a 40 h/wk savings claim, the single largest quantified promise on the landing. NUMBER_CLAIM at audit-public-claims.ts:214 matches only `\d+ (seconds|minutes|hours|days|federal data sources)`, `$\d+`, `\d+%`, and `\d{3}–\d{3}`. "62 hr" contains "hr", not "hours", so the completeness pass never sees it; nor does it see the bare counts on the same column ("14 overnight replies", "22 leads comped", "37 of 37"). The population is also predicate-limited in a second way: the pass only looks for NUMBERS, so every non-numeric capability claim — Quotes.tsx's six mechanic statements, ten of Features.tsx's twelve cards — is outside the gate entirely. Fourteen registered claims stand against roughly sixty claim-sentences on the surface.
- **Fix:** Extend NUMBER_CLAIM to hr/hrs/week/wk and to bare counts adjacent to a noun; more importantly, add a per-file claim-sentence floor so a section that renders prose must have at least one registered claim, and register or delete the hour figures. If the hours stay, they need a labeled basis ("illustrative composite, not measured") the way the hero fixtures do.
- **Comparable:** Any regulated-adjacent SaaS (Ramp, Brex) treats a quantified outcome claim as the highest-scrutiny copy on the page, with a named study or a customer cohort behind it. AcreOS has neither yet — customers.md shows "Active paying (0)" and "Trial in flight (0)", last updated 2026-05-29.
- **Evidence:** Read scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:85-215 (CLAIMS list and EXEMPT map) and line 214 verbatim; read DayInLife.tsx in full; read customers.md:1-45.

#### [high/S] The audit's backing source for the positioning claim describes a vertical tiering the page stopped rendering on 2026-08-17

- **Where:** `scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:292-306 vs shared/business-types/publicClaims.ts:77-89`
- **Defect:** The source paragraph named "AcreOS business types registry" asserts the landing renders "core (land flipper, note investor — full workflow templates), beta (residential wholesaler, buy-and-hold rentals, subdivider, tax lien / deed), and roadmap (fix-and-flip, short-term rentals, commercial, creative finance, developer, multifamily, mobile home, agent-investor)". After the OD-5 demotions the page renders the near-inverse: core = fix_and_flip + subdivider, beta = the other twelve including land flipper and note investor, roadmap = empty. It further asserts "deepest in land is a self-referential depth fact backed by this registry's core maturity" — land's public maturity is beta. So the truth engine verifies the live claim "labeled by what's true today" against a source that is itself false, in CI, green. This is the same defect class the file's header at lines 6-13 says it was reworked to eliminate.
- **Fix:** Derive the source paragraph from deriveLandingTiers() at audit time rather than hand-writing it, so a demotion change cannot leave a stale source behind. At minimum, correct it and add a test that the source's named tiers equal the rendered tiers.
- **Comparable:** n/a — this is an internal-truth defect, not a market one, but it is the mechanism that lets findings 1 and 3 survive CI.
- **Evidence:** Read scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:292-306 verbatim; read shared/business-types/publicClaims.ts:55-95; read Positioning.tsx deriveLandingTiers.

#### [high/S] The Agents section renders illustrative ledger figures with no "Example" label, and the audit exempts them on the stated grounds that they carry one

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing/Agents.tsx:34-40, 61-67, 76-86; scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:200-202`
- **Defect:** Agents.tsx renders three sample cards headed "Pax just finished APN 304-12-456", "Pax has 4 drafts ready for review" and "Pax ledger · this week" with rows "Payments collected $14,820 / Notes serviced 37 of 37 / Receipts sent 37 / Late notices 2 (waiting for your tap)". Nothing on the card says it is an example. Hero.tsx:150-168 defines HeroExampleChip and ProductShots.tsx:25-27 defines ExampleChip; Agents.tsx imports neither and the string "Example" does not occur in the file. audit-public-claims.ts:200-202 exempts the "$11" token with the reason "Agents.tsx sample cards — illustrative rows on the 'Pax just finished / Pax ledger' sample cards (same contract as the hero fixtures)" — an exemption granted on the basis of a visible label that is not rendered. The audit header itself records that this exact section printed "37 servicing receipts sent overnight" from no row and was caught once already.
- **Fix:** Import the same chip into Agents.tsx (one line per card), and make the exemption mechanical: an EXEMPT entry citing the fixture contract should only be honored when the file actually contains the chip component. Also drop "Pulls 10–20 comparables for any APN" (Agents.tsx:34) — copy.ts:44-46 retired the "14 comps per parcel" claim for exactly this reason and the engine returns whatever the provider finds.
- **Comparable:** Stripe's product screenshots use obviously synthetic names and a persistent "sample data" treatment. AcreOS already does this correctly twice (Hero, ProductShots) — the defect is that the third instance was missed.
- **Evidence:** Read Agents.tsx in full (no 'Example' string anywhere); read Hero.tsx:150-168 and ProductShots.tsx:24-27; read scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:178-213.

#### [high/S] The FAQ names two note-servicing migration paths that exist nowhere in the codebase

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing/FAQ.tsx:31 (and :15)`
- **Defect:** FAQ.tsx:31: "AcreOS imports and services notes from Beanstalk, Note Servicing Center, or a CSV. Migration support is included on a 30-min call." A case-insensitive grep for "beanstalk" and "note servicing center" across client/, server/ and shared/ returns exactly one hit: this sentence. server/services/importExport.ts uses a generic lowercase column map plus a user-supplied fieldMap (lines 211-263, 1061) — there are no vendor presets for anything. FAQ.tsx:15 has the milder version of the same problem for PropStream/REISift/Pebble/DataTree, softened by "or any source." Separately, "Migration support is included on a 30-min call" is an unbounded human commitment from a solo operation, and Scale-tier copy compounds it (see the pricing finding).
- **Fix:** Either ship two real column presets (Beanstalk and NSC export headers are public and small) and keep the sentence, or say "Import notes from any CSV — we map your columns during setup." A named-vendor claim on a public page should require a preset in the repo; that is a one-line test.
- **Comparable:** Attio and Ramp list named importers only where a tested mapping ships, and route everything else to a generic CSV path. Naming a competitor system is read by buyers as "they have tested this with my data."
- **Evidence:** Grepped case-insensitively for beanstalk / 'note servicing center' across client/src, server, shared — one hit, FAQ.tsx:31; grepped server/services/importExport.ts for preset/mapping/columnMap (generic map only).

#### [high/M] The price ceiling sits below every competitor's floor, and the top tier promises human service $79/mo cannot fund

- **Where:** `shared/billing/tier-pricing.ts:73-113; client/src/lib/pricing-copy.ts:196-210; shared/billing/tier-limits.ts:96-108`
- **Defect:** AcreOS charges $20/$49/$79 while claiming to replace the CRM, the data tool, the mail platform, the dialer, the note servicer and the spreadsheet. In this category price is a quality signal: a buyer paying REsimpli $299 reads $79 as a hobby tool, and the landing's own "replaces 7 tools" framing makes the gap louder, not quieter. Two second-order harms are already visible in the repo. (a) Unit economics: tier-limits.ts:96-108 records Starter's worst-case AI COGS at ≈$11.25 of $20 — "56% of revenue — thin, watch it" — before mail, data or support. At $79 max ARPU there is no room to buy a customer in a market where DealMachine spends at scale. (b) Promise/capacity: pricing-copy.ts:196-205 sells Scale ($79/mo, sales-assisted via a mailto) with "Dedicated success partner", "White-glove migration" and "Quarterly portfolio review" — four hours of human time a year exceeds the plan's annual revenue.
- **Fix:** Reprice before the first cohort, not after: land the wedge tier at $99-$149 to sit inside the category's consideration band, and either delete the Scale human-service bullets or move them behind a real quoted tier. The 30-day money-back guarantee (terms.tsx §5A) is the risk-reversal that makes a higher price safe to test.
- **Comparable:** REsimpli $149/$299/$599 (docs/strategy/competitors/reipro-resimpli.md:115-117), DealMachine $99/$149/$232 plus $0.57-0.67/piece mail (dealmachine.md:108-114), REIPro $109 (reipro-resimpli.md:104). AcreOS's most expensive plan is 47% below the cheapest plan any of them sell.
- **Evidence:** Read shared/billing/tier-pricing.ts:70-115 and shared/billing/tier-limits.ts:90-140; read client/src/lib/pricing-copy.ts in full; read docs/strategy/competitors/reipro-resimpli.md:104-126 and dealmachine.md:108-126.

#### [high/M] First-run still celebrates sample data — the exact thing the roadmap's activation wave said to stop celebrating — while the product's best cold-start asset sits unused

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/onboarding-v2.tsx:1030-1070, 1155-1160; docs/company/roadmap-2026-07.md:167-170`
- **Defect:** Step 2's primary action is a large card labeled "Try with sample data" carrying a "Recommended" badge (onboarding-v2.tsx:1040-1069); "Import a CSV instead" and "Set up data later" are low-weight text links (:1148-1160). roadmap-2026-07.md:167-170 (Wave 2, "Win the wedge (activation)") explicitly says: "onboarding celebrates loading sample data instead of sending a first offer… the parcel → blind-offer → hand-to-Pax flow already exists on the Map — celebrate *that*, not the sample-data load." The finish destination was moved to /maps for land_flipper, but the step that decides activation was not. Nothing in the wizard offers to run a real parcel — the one thing that proves the product on the user's own dirt in ~30 seconds and requires no data from them at all.
- **Fix:** Make step 2's primary action "Check a parcel you're looking at" (address input → the existing public parcel-check pipeline, written into their workspace as their first property, which is already a first-value event). Demote sample data to the secondary link for people who have nothing to type. This also makes the landing's secondary CTA and the first-run aha the same object, which is what the 10-second test needs.
- **Comparable:** DealMachine's activation is "drive a street and we pull the owner" — the user's own data in under a minute. Carrot's is your own site live on your own domain. Both make the first artifact THEIRS. AcreOS already owns the equivalent: a no-signup streaming parcel check that resolves five federal sources on a real address (server/routes-public-parcel-check.ts).
- **Evidence:** Read onboarding-v2.tsx:1-110 and 1030-1170; read client/src/lib/onboarding-verticals.ts:50-120; read docs/company/roadmap-2026-07.md:159-183; confirmed server/routes-public-parcel-check.ts is the no-signup path referenced by Hero.tsx cta2.

#### [medium/S] /why is a stranded public surface contradicting three live decisions, and it is linked from the landing footer

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/why.tsx:47-77; linked from client/src/pages/landing/Footer.tsx:73`
- **Defect:** why.tsx is a first-person founder letter: "I'm a land investor…", "If you're running a land business and the seams are starting to show, this is built for you", and "It runs in the background. You stay on the decisions only you should make." All three are now contradicted. copy.ts's header states the founder-letter voice and the FounderNote section were removed deliberately; founder ruling #12(a) repositioned the audience from land to property investors; and the 2026-09-02 controls ruling makes "runs in the background" the opposite of the posture the FAQ now advertises. The page is reachable from the landing footer's Learn column, so a prospect who clicks "Why AcreOS" after reading "for property investors" is told the product is for land businesses.
- **Fix:** Either rewrite /why to the current voice and audience (it is 90 lines), or retire the route and point the footer link at the Positioning section. Do not leave a public page carrying a retired thesis.
- **Comparable:** n/a — this is hygiene. Linear's about page and marketing page use one voice and one audience noun; a stranded founder letter reads as an abandoned company.
- **Evidence:** Read client/src/pages/why.tsx in full; read client/src/pages/landing/Footer.tsx:66-76; read copy.ts:1-30 header.

#### [medium/S] The repositioning to "property investors" never reached the meta descriptions of the highest-intent public pages

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/pricing.tsx:173; client/src/pages/security.tsx:111; client/src/pages/changelog.tsx:82`
- **Defect:** Founder ruling #12(a) (2026-07-29) repositioned AcreOS from land investors to property investors, and the landing was updated. Five weeks later, /pricing — the page a purchase-intent searcher lands on — still ships description="Transparent plans for Land Investors…" (pricing.tsx:173). /security says "How AcreOS protects Land Investors' data" (security.tsx:111). /changelog says "written for Land Investors using the platform" (changelog.tsx:82). These are the strings Google renders and Slack/iMessage unfurl, so a repositioned company still introduces itself with the old audience in three of its public entry points.
- **Fix:** Sweep the public page meta for the audience noun and add it to the landing-surface population that scripts/audit-public-claims.ts already reads, so an audience string outside the approved set fails CI rather than aging quietly.
- **Comparable:** Every well-run SaaS treats the audience noun as a single token. Stripe changed "for developers" to "for businesses" across every surface in one pass, including OG cards, because the SERP snippet is the first impression more often than the page is.
- **Evidence:** Grepped client/src/pages/*.tsx and compare/*.tsx for 'Land Investors' inside meta/description/title calls — four hits, three of them public pages.

#### [medium/S] The landing's own SEO/social description promises "AI agents that act on your behalf" — the exact thing the founder ruled out and the glossary bans

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing.tsx:62 and :75; client/src/pages/landing/Features.tsx:31; client/src/pages/landing/Quotes.tsx:43`
- **Defect:** usePageDescription (landing.tsx:62) and the OpenGraph description (:75) both end "…track every deal through closing with AI agents that act on your behalf." That is the SERP snippet and the share-card copy — the most-read sentence AcreOS ships — and it promises unattended action, which founder question 1 answered NO on 2026-09-02 (AUTONOMY_SPEC.md §7) and which shared/pax-controls.ts:44 enforces by allowing only two ask-first stances. It also uses the plural "agents," which contradicts the single-Pax persona architecture documented in Agents.tsx's own header. Features.tsx:31 ("Every agent action") and Quotes.tsx:43 ("attributed to operator or agent") carry the same noun into the body copy; neither file was in wave 1's copy set.
- **Fix:** Rewrite both descriptions to the true and stronger claim — "…with Pax, an AI partner that drafts everything and sends nothing until you approve it" — and replace "agent" with "Pax" in Features.tsx:31 and Quotes.tsx:43. Then extend the wave-1 glossary scan's population to include Features.tsx and Quotes.tsx, which it currently does not cover.
- **Comparable:** n/a — vocabulary coherence. Anthropic's own product surfaces and Zendesk's AI copy both went through a deliberate noun consolidation for the same reason: two names for one thing costs a buyer a beat, and "agent" carries an autonomy connotation this product deliberately rejects.
- **Evidence:** Read client/src/pages/landing.tsx:55-80; grepped landing/*.tsx for 'agent' outside comments; read docs/autonomous/AUTONOMY_SPEC.md §2 banned-vocabulary line and §7 question 1; read shared/pax-controls.ts:36-56.

#### [medium/S] The two highest-commercial-intent pages in the funnel are built and orphaned

- **Where:** `client/src/App.tsx:750-751 (/compare/acreos-vs-propstream, /compare/acreos-vs-dealmachine); client/src/pages/landing/LandingNav.tsx:15-19; Footer.tsx:56-90`
- **Defect:** Both comparison pages exist as routes with a shared SEO shell (pages/compare/ComparisonPage.tsx, whose header states they target "propstream alternative" / "dealmachine vs" traffic). A grep for links to /compare across client/src returns only App.tsx's route registrations. LandingNav.tsx ships three anchors (#how, #agents, #pricing) and no compare link; Footer.tsx has Product / Free tools / Learn / Contact columns and no compare link — despite the footer's own comment (lines 56-62) explaining that it was restructured precisely to de-orphan parcel-check and the Land Credit Score. The comparison pages were missed in that same pass, so they collect neither internal PageRank nor click-through from the one page that has traffic.
- **Fix:** Add a "Switching from" line to the footer Product column and a compare link into the FAQ's import answer. Then write the third page the category demands (vs REsimpli) — it is the closest competitor to AcreOS's actual scope and the only one whose bundle overlaps the wedge end to end.
- **Comparable:** REsimpli's own 2026 anti-PropStream positioning campaign is documented in this repo at docs/strategy/competitors/reipro-resimpli.md:221. "X vs Y" is the highest-converting organic page type in this category, and every incumbent runs them from primary navigation.
- **Evidence:** Grepped client/src for '/compare' outside pages/compare — only App.tsx:750-751; read LandingNav.tsx and Footer.tsx in full; read docs/strategy/competitors/reipro-resimpli.md:215-225.

#### [medium/L] The content moat is thirteen files, and the engine that would scale it is built and switched off

- **Where:** `content/learn/** (13 JSON files); docs/company/free-distribution-playbook.md:22-42`
- **Defect:** content/learn holds 5 note-investing state primers, 5 land-flipping state primers, and 3 county pages (arizona/navajo, arizona/cochise, texas/brewster). The quality is genuinely high — the Georgia and Texas primers cite O.C.G.A. §44-14-162, Tex. Prop. Code §5.077 with the $250/day exposure math, and read like a practitioner wrote them — which makes the thinness the problem rather than the craft. free-distribution-playbook.md:36-42 says the county-guide engine "drafts grounded content daily but publishes nothing until you flip the switch," and the playbook's first three founder actions (Search Console, flip auto-publish, directory listings) are one-time, low-hour, and evidently not done. With zero paying customers (customers.md), organic content is the only acquisition channel the DO-NOT-DO list leaves open at this stage — paid ads are founder-instrument-only and there is no marketplace or API.
- **Fix:** Treat this as the primary growth workstream, not a side asset: turn on auto-publish behind the existing witnessed gate, set a weekly county-page target, and pick the 20 counties the product already has data depth in. Link /learn from the primary nav's overflow, not only the footer.
- **Comparable:** Carrot's entire business is organic real-estate-investor content and investor sites; REsimpli and DealMachine both run hundreds of state/county pages. Thirteen pages is not a moat, it is a sample.
- **Evidence:** Listed content/learn recursively (13 files) and read three primers in full; read docs/company/free-distribution-playbook.md:1-60; read customers.md:24-40 (Active paying (0), Trial in flight (0), last updated 2026-05-29).

#### [medium/S] An in-product empty state carries a fabricated latency claim for a feature the reader's tier cannot use

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/listings.tsx:627; server/routes-team-messaging.ts:1043-1082, 1202`
- **Defect:** The listings empty state reads "Publish your first listing — Pax syndicates it to your active channels within 90 seconds and threads every inquiry back to the parcel." Syndication is operator-triggered (POST /api/listings/:id/publish, routes-team-messaging.ts:1043), not something Pax does; it is gated to Scale and above (:1076-1082 plan-tier gate on marketplace_syndication, with an explicit "Marketplace syndication requires a Scale plan or higher" refusal at :1202); and no 90-second SLA exists anywhere. This is exactly the claim shape the truth sweep removed from the landing on 2026-09-01, still live in the product — which tells me the landing population (11 files) is the whole audited surface while ~300 customer-facing pages are unscanned.
- **Fix:** Rewrite to the true mechanic and the true gate. More importantly, extend the number-bearing claim scan beyond client/src/pages/landing/** to all customer-facing empty states and marketing-toned copy — that is where the next fabricated number will be.
- **Comparable:** n/a — this is the same defect class the landing already fixed on 2026-09-01, appearing one layer in.
- **Evidence:** Read client/src/pages/listings.tsx:600-660; grepped server for 'syndicat' and read routes-team-messaging.ts:1043-1090 and 1195-1260; read scripts/audit-public-claims.ts:36-66 (LANDING_FILES population).

#### [medium/S] A trust claim on the landing self-expires in 26 days with no owner

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing/FinalCTA.tsx:86; client/src/pages/security.tsx:342-352`
- **Defect:** The final CTA's trust strip prints "SOC 2 Type 1 in progress · 2026-Q3 target". Today is 2026-09-04; Q3 ends 2026-09-30. The security page (security.tsx:342-352) carries the same status with no date and softer language ("actively pursuing", "audited continuously"), so the landing is the only surface making the commitment. If it slips — and nothing in the repo shows an engaged auditor or an observation window — the most trust-sensitive sentence on the page becomes provably stale in front of exactly the enterprise-adjacent prospects it was written for.
- **Fix:** Drop the date from the landing and keep the status; or set a calendar owner and a review date. A dated compliance claim should have the same expiry-check treatment as the EXEMPT entries in the claims audit.
- **Comparable:** Vanta and Stripe both publish compliance status without a public target date, or with a date they control. A dated promise on a marketing page is a liability the moment it slips.
- **Evidence:** Read client/src/pages/landing/FinalCTA.tsx:80-92 and client/src/pages/security.tsx:330-378; no auditor engagement or observation-window record found under docs/company.

#### [low/M] Fourteen sections, three of which do the same job — the page is long where it should be sharp

- **Where:** `client/src/pages/landing.tsx:85-99 (section order); ProductShots.tsx / Agents.tsx / Quotes.tsx`
- **Defect:** The page renders Hero → ProductShots → Positioning → HowItWorks → Agents → DayInLife → Features → LandCreditScore → DataProvenance → Quotes → Pricing → FAQ → FinalCTA. ProductShots (rendered app frames), Agents (three tabs of Pax capabilities with sample cards) and Quotes (six mechanic statements) are three different answers to "what does Pax do," and Features adds twelve more cards on top. Meanwhile the wedge sentence (copy.ts:79) appears exactly once and never again — not in the mid-page, not at the CTA (FinalCTA's title is the generic "Try AcreOS for two weeks"). The Positioning band, whose own header says it belongs "between Hero and HowItWorks" to answer "is this for me?", has been pushed below ProductShots.
- **Fix:** Cut to Hero → Positioning → HowItWorks → ProductShots → Features → LandCreditScore/Data → Pricing → FAQ → CTA. Fold Agents into ProductShots (the Pax thread frame already does that job) and retire Quotes. Repeat the wedge sentence verbatim in the FinalCTA title.
- **Comparable:** Linear, Stripe and Attio all run 6-8 sections and repeat one sentence — the promise — at the top, the middle and the CTA. The 10-second test is passed by repetition of one idea, not by coverage of every idea.
- **Evidence:** Read client/src/pages/landing.tsx in full and all 15 section files; compared the rendered order against Positioning.tsx's own header comment.

## CTO — architecture, seams, coupling, build/deploy, observability, test architecture, and two-year change absorption

**The governance layer (ratchets, reachability lint, falsification-in-gate) is genuinely world-class, but it is installed over a runtime whose structural seams have dissolved — 273 route modules and 1,725 handlers hand-mounted in one file, 728 tables in a 17,967-line schema, four competing job systems, two competing data-brokers, zero ESLint on the entire server, and a tenant-isolation lint whose population excludes every route file, which is where I found a live cross-tenant read of another org's leads.**


### Already at or above the bar — do not "fix" these

- The ratchet system (scripts/ratchet.mjs + 17 configs) with VACUITY FLOORS on the scan population, checked BEFORE the baseline comparison. 'This gate counts BAD THINGS FOUND, so a scan that stops seeing files finds zero and reports it as progress' is a sharper articulation of gate failure than most elite teams ever write down, and the `minima` mechanism is the fix. Do not touch this; steal from it.
- scripts/lint-reachability.mjs — 'built but unwired' converted from a recurring manual audit into CI, with six independently-baselined families (unreached exports, internal-only exports, tables-no-writer, tables-no-reader, unregistered routes, module orphans) and eight named historical instances in the header explaining why each family exists. I have not seen this gate anywhere else. Findings 14 and 18 are about paying the balance down, not about the gate.
- Falsification-in-gate: tests/unit/paxToolsReportRealEffects.test.ts mutates the real historical defect back in — the code exactly as it shipped, not a synthetic stand-in — and asserts the gate fires. Applying mutation-testing discipline to policy gates rather than to unit tests is the right instinct and it is rare. Finding 16 is about widening its population, not weakening it.
- server/jobs/jobRegistry.ts — a deadman roster derived from `withJobLock` literals, with the correct insight that the liveness cadence is the wall-clock work cadence and not the setInterval tick. The header's diagnosis (all three existing health systems key on rows that EXIST, not rows that SHOULD exist) is exactly right and is the reason a job going dark is detectable at all.
- server/middleware/limiterRedisStore.ts — correct multi-instance rate limiting, an explicitly documented degrade path, a fail-open-per-call policy, and `limiterStoreBackend()` exported as a pure function so backend selection is testable without Redis. This is how the whole codebase should read.
- server/utils/logger.ts — structured logging with W3C trace/span IDs auto-stamped from AsyncLocalStorage (no request threading) and PII redaction on every line, shared with the Sentry beforeSend hook. The `orgId`/`organizationId` alias with a stated canonical preference is a pragmatic, honest compromise.
- The Dockerfile's supply-chain discipline — `gh` compiled from source against a pinned patched Go toolchain rather than the apt package, with the CVE history and the diagnosis procedure written inline; non-root user; healthcheck; separate build-stage and runtime heap ceilings with the reason each exists. Better than most Series-B production images.
- deploy.yml's pre-deploy image baseline (`FLY_IMAGE_SET_JQ` defined once and shared by the baseline and post-deploy assessment, so the two cannot disagree) and a rollback that re-promotes that exact image, making rollback idempotent. The comment explaining why the old rollback condition was wrong is the kind of thing that prevents the same outage twice.
- `withJobLock` in server/utils/jobRuntime.ts — a Postgres-backed lock with an instance-scoped holder UUID, released in a `finally`, with skipped_lock and failure both recorded. The locking semantics are correct; finding 10 is about the scheduler wrapped around it, not this.
- Comment and decision quality throughout. Nearly every non-obvious choice carries a date, the failure that motivated it, and what was measured. scripts/ratchets/table-count.json's `lastBumpNote` is a small essay justifying a single new table against nine alternatives. This institutional memory in-repo is genuinely uncommon and is the main reason this codebase is still tractable at its size — protect it, and require it of every wave.

### Benchmarked against

- Stripe — one and only one error-envelope producer, `request_id` on every response including uncaught 5xx, and a tenant handle that makes an unscoped query untypeable. Directly applicable to findings 1, 15.
- Linear / Vercel — sub-10-minute PR feedback via a single `workflow_call` reusable job plus test sharding and affected-graph selection; the deploy gate `needs:` CI rather than re-running it. Directly applicable to finding 5.
- Shopify (packwerk) — layer boundaries as first-class, ratchetable configuration ('routes may not import the database'), enforced by the same scanner that already exists here. Directly applicable to findings 19, 18.
- Temporal / Inngest — one durable job runtime with one retry and DLQ policy, and registration derived from a registry so a job cannot exist outside it. AcreOS already has the registry (jobRegistry.ts) and the good runtime (scheduler.ts); it just has three more. Finding 10.
- Supabase / Atlas (ariga) — schema drift proven on every PR by migrating an ephemeral Postgres and diffing against the declared schema, replacing both the 11k-line SQL array and the table-level mirror lint. Findings 4, 6.
- Honeycomb / Datadog — the tracing SDK is a hard dependency and the process refuses to boot when export is configured but unavailable, so a silently-dead tracer cannot exist. Finding 7.
- dbt semantic layer / Cube — one metric, one definition, and a second implementation of a named metric is a build failure. Finding 9.
- Semgrep / CodeQL — security and tenancy rules whose population is the compiled program, not a hand-maintained glob; this is the structural answer to the repo's recurring 'the gate never opened that file' failure. Findings 2, 16.
- Next.js / Remix file-system routing, or NestJS module encapsulation — mount order as a declared property of a module rather than a line position in a 2,721-line function. Finding 13.

### Findings


#### [critical/S] Cross-tenant read: /api/onboarding/instant-deal-hunt returns other organizations' leads

- **Where:** `server/routes-onboarding.ts:272-326 (query at 288-298); mounted server/routes.ts:1537`
- **Defect:** The handler selects from `leads` filtered ONLY by `eq(leads.state, state)` and `or(isNull(leads.county), eq(leads.county, county))` — there is no `organizationId` predicate anywhere in the query, and `leads.organizationId` is declared `.notNull()` in shared/schema.ts. It orders by `desc(leads.score)` limit 10 and returns the top 5 with `ownerName`, `acreage`, `assessedValue`, a computed `motivationScore`/`motivationGrade`, `estimatedOfferPrice`, `estimatedResaleValue` and `potentialProfit`, labelled `source: "live_database"`. The router is mounted with `isAuthenticated, getOrCreateOrg`, so any authenticated paying customer can pass an arbitrary county/state and receive another organization's highest-scoring acquisition pipeline — owner identities and the offer prices AcreOS computed for a competitor. This is the customer's single most proprietary asset. The surrounding code in the same file is scrupulous about scoping (line 209 and 211 both carry `eq(leads.organizationId, orgId)`), and the fallback branch below was deliberately rewritten to refuse rather than fabricate — so this is an isolated miss, not a house style.
- **Fix:** Add `eq(leads.organizationId, getOrganizationId(req))` to the `and(...)` at line 292. Then make the class impossible: route this read through the org-scoped repository layer (`forOrg()` in server/utils/orgScopedDb.ts) rather than a bare `db.select()` in a route file, and add the file to the org-scope lint population (finding 2).
- **Comparable:** Stripe and Linear both make the tenant key structurally unforgeable rather than conventionally applied — every query goes through a scoped handle that carries the account/workspace, so a bare `db.select()` in a request handler does not typecheck. Postgres RLS is the other standard answer and applies directly here.
- **Evidence:** Read server/routes-onboarding.ts:272-326 in full; confirmed `leads.organizationId ... .notNull()` by parsing the `leads` pgTable body out of shared/schema.ts; confirmed the mount and its middleware at server/routes.ts:1537.

#### [high/M] The tenant-isolation lint's population excludes all 273 route files, all jobs, all AI code, and the entire Drizzle relational-query API

- **Where:** `scripts/check-org-scoped-fetch.mjs (1,909 lines; scan roots stated at :66)`
- **Defect:** The lint walks `server/storage.ts`, `server/storage/*.ts` and `server/services/**` — and nothing else. `grep -c 'db\.query' scripts/check-org-scoped-fetch.mjs` returns 0, while `server/` contains 280 `db.query.<table>.findMany/findFirst` call sites, so the whole relational API is outside the syntax it recognises. 92 of the 273 `server/routes-*.ts` files import `db` directly and 51 call `db.select(` — none of that is scanned. My crude scan of just the route files found 117 candidate org-scoped reads with a `.where(` that mentions no org key; most sampled turned out to be founder-gated or FK-derived, but finding 1 is a real one, sitting precisely inside this blind spot. CLAUDE.md's third law names this exact gap ("the org-scope lint was found blind to Drizzle's relational-query API") and it is still open. The lint's own header records the same lesson twice before ("the defect was never a missing rule, only a rule applied to some surfaces and not others"), so this is the third repetition of a pattern the repo has already written down.
- **Fix:** Extend globs to `server/routes-*.ts`, `server/routes/**`, `server/jobs/**`, `server/ai/**`. Add a `db.query.<table>.find*` extractor alongside the existing `.select().from()` one. Then add the per-population vacuity assertion the constitution demands — one floor per glob group, so a glob that silently matches zero route files fails instead of reading as compliance. Re-baseline against whatever the widened scan finds and triage down.
- **Comparable:** Semgrep/CodeQL taint rules are the industry answer here because the population is the whole compiled program by construction, not a hand-maintained glob. GitHub's own CodeQL 'missing tenant filter' queries are the closest analogue.
- **Evidence:** Read the header and grepped the file for `db.query` (0 hits) and for its scan roots; counted `db.query.*.find*` across server/ (280) and route files importing db (92) / calling db.select( (51); ran a scripted candidate scan over server/routes-*.ts against the 182 org-scoped table vars parsed out of shared/schema*.

#### [high/M] The entire server has zero ESLint coverage

- **Where:** `eslint.config.js:44 (`"server/**"` in `ignores`); package.json:41 (`"lint": "eslint client/src --max-warnings 0"`)`
- **Defect:** 1,499 server TypeScript files — every one of the 1,725 HTTP handlers, the money paths, the auth middleware, the job runtime — are explicitly excluded from linting. There is no `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises`, `no-misused-promises`, `require-await`, or `no-unsafe-argument` anywhere on the backend. This is the root cause of the repo's ~30 bespoke `scripts/check-*.mjs` regex linters: each one re-implements, over a hand-written file glob, a check a typed linter would do over the real program graph. That is exactly the fragile-population failure mode CLAUDE.md's third law describes, and the repo keeps paying for it (findings 2 and 16). It also means the codebase's heavy fire-and-forget style (`db.insert(...).catch(() => {})` in server/utils/jobRuntime.ts:52, `void (async () => …)()` at server/index.ts:796) has no automated check that a promise which *should* be awaited isn't being dropped.
- **Fix:** Turn on `typescript-eslint` type-aware linting for `server/**` with a zero-new-violations ratchet (the repo already has this exact machinery in scripts/lint-eslint-ratchet.mjs and eslint-rules-baseline.json — extend it rather than build it). Start with four rules: no-floating-promises, no-misused-promises, require-await, no-unnecessary-condition. Then retire the bespoke scanners that a typed rule subsumes.
- **Comparable:** Vercel, Shopify and Linear all run type-aware ESLint over the server with `no-floating-promises` as a hard error; it is the single rule that most reliably catches the swallowed-background-work class this codebase has repeatedly shipped.
- **Evidence:** Read eslint.config.js:23-48 (the ignores array) and package.json's `lint` script; counted server .ts files (1,499) and HTTP handlers (1,725 `router|app.(get|post|put|patch|delete)(` across server/).

#### [high/M] Column-level schema drift reaches production with no gate; the deploy's own migration check globs a directory that does not exist

- **Where:** `scripts/migrate.mjs (11,248 lines, fly.toml `release_command`); scripts/check-schema-migrate-mirror.mjs:56-60; .github/workflows/deploy.yml:159-162`
- **Defect:** Three compounding problems. (a) The authoritative production DDL path is an 11,248-line hand-maintained array of idempotent SQL strings; drizzle.config.ts states outright that the migrator is deliberately not used and that `migrations/meta/_journal.json` was deleted — and indeed `migrations/meta/` holds four snapshots and no journal. (b) check-schema-migrate-mirror.mjs is TABLE-level only; its own header says column drift is "left to the DB-backed `migrate.mjs --dry-run` gate (which needs a live DB, so it runs in the deploy pipeline, not here)" — but `grep -rn 'dry-run' .github/workflows/` returns zero hits. That gate is not wired anywhere. So adding a column to shared/schema.ts and forgetting the ALTER ships a 500 for all tenants, exactly the incident migrate.mjs's own comments record repeatedly. (c) deploy.yml:162 runs `find drizzle -name "*.sql" -newer fly.toml` — there is no `drizzle/` directory in this repo (migrations live in `migrations/`), so MIGRATION_COUNT is permanently 0 and the step is decorative.
- **Fix:** Wire `node scripts/migrate.mjs --dry-run` into deploy.yml's `test` job against the postgres:16 service container that job already provisions — it is the only thing standing between a schema-only edit and a production 500. Fix or delete deploy.yml:159-162. Longer-term: rebuild a clean drizzle journal from a single baseline snapshot so there is one migration path instead of two-and-a-half.
- **Comparable:** Supabase and PlanetScale both gate on a diff between the declared schema and a freshly-migrated ephemeral database on every PR. Atlas (ariga/atlas) does exactly this for a Drizzle-shaped project and would replace both the 11k-line array and the mirror lint.
- **Evidence:** Read scripts/migrate.mjs:1-60, drizzle.config.ts in full, scripts/check-schema-migrate-mirror.mjs:1-63, deploy.yml:159-162; `ls migrations/meta/` (4 snapshots, no _journal.json); grepped all workflows for `dry-run` (0 hits).

#### [high/M] CI runs the full 11,305-test suite five times per merge across four duplicate workflows

- **Where:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml:78 + :92, test.yml:66, deploy.yml:73, staging.yml:59`
- **Defect:** On a push to main, nine workflows fire; four of them (ci, test, deploy, staging) each do `npm ci` → `npm run check` → a full `npx vitest run`, and ci.yml then runs the entire suite a *second* time under v8 coverage instrumentation. `npm run check` is itself 28 sequential commands — `tsc --noEmit --incremental false` at a 6 GB heap, `check:tests` over a superset program, then 26 bespoke lints — with no parallelism. There is no vitest sharding, no `workflow_call` reuse, and deploy.yml re-runs the gate rather than `needs:`-ing CI's result. On a PR, three of those four still run. This is the single biggest tax on iteration speed in the repo, and it compounds with the wave model: six parallel agents each pushing checkpoints multiplies it.
- **Fix:** Extract one reusable `workflow_call` job (install + check + test) and have ci/test/deploy/staging all `uses:` it. Shard vitest with `--shard=i/N` across a 4-way matrix. Split `npm run check` into two parallel jobs (typecheck vs. the 26 lints — they share nothing). Drop the duplicate plain `vitest run` in ci.yml and keep only the coverage run, which already produces the same pass/fail signal.
- **Comparable:** Linear and Vercel both hold PR feedback under 10 minutes with a single reusable workflow plus test sharding and affected-graph selection (Turborepo / Nx `affected`). Running the suite once and reusing the artifact is table stakes.
- **Evidence:** Read all four workflow files and enumerated every `on:` trigger across .github/workflows/ (9 fire on push to main); read package.json's `check` script (28 chained commands); counted 11,305 `it(`/`test(` blocks across tests/.

#### [high/M] The CI test database is provisioned but never migrated — `drizzle-kit migrate` cannot run

- **Where:** `.github/workflows/test.yml:14-32 (postgres:16 service + DATABASE_URL) and :58-60`
- **Defect:** test.yml stands up a real Postgres 16 service container and exports DATABASE_URL, then runs `npx drizzle-kit migrate` with `continue-on-error: true` and the comment "migrations may not exist in test env". `migrations/meta/_journal.json` does not exist (drizzle.config.ts documents its deliberate deletion), so that step always fails and is always swallowed — the schema is never created. Every test therefore runs against an empty database that *looks* provisioned. This is why 219 of 831 unit-test files mock `server/db` and only 26 files in the entire repo use supertest against 1,725 endpoints. The global coverage floor in vitest.config.ts:45 is `lines: 18`. So the suite's 11,305 assertions largely certify pure functions and source-text shapes, not the behaviour of the system as deployed.
- **Fix:** Replace the drizzle-kit step with `node scripts/migrate.mjs` (the actual production path) against the service container, and make it a hard failure. That single change simultaneously turns on finding 4's missing column-drift gate and unlocks real integration tests. Then set a per-directory coverage floor for `tests/integration/**` and start moving the highest-risk route families (auth, billing, leads) to supertest-against-real-Postgres.
- **Comparable:** Testcontainers-per-suite is the norm at Stripe/Shopify scale; Supabase's own CI migrates a throwaway Postgres on every PR. The rule an elite team applies: if CI provisions a database, a test must fail when the database is empty.
- **Evidence:** Read test.yml:14-68; `ls migrations/meta/` (0000-0003 snapshots, no _journal.json); read drizzle.config.ts's note on the journal deletion; counted supertest files (26), db-mocking unit tests (219), and read vitest.config.ts's thresholds block.

#### [high/M] Distributed tracing is permanently dead code that fails silently, and /metrics is scraped by nothing

- **Where:** `server/tracing.ts:34-48 (bare `try { require(...) } catch {}`); server/index.ts:687; server/index.ts:681 (`app.get("/metrics", metricsHandler)`)`
- **Defect:** tracing.ts `require()`s `@opentelemetry/instrumentation-http` and `@opentelemetry/instrumentation-express` — neither is installed nor present in package.json (0 of 164 dependencies match `opentelemetry`; both directories are absent from node_modules, as is `exporter-trace-otlp-http`). It also does `require("@opentelemetry/resources").Resource`, but the transitively-present version is 2.6.1, where `Resource` is no longer an exported class. All of this sits in a bare `catch {}` at :47 that leaves every binding `undefined`, and `initTracing()`'s only caller wraps it in a second catch that logs "Tracing init skipped". So the 230-line module cannot work in any configuration, and its failure is invisible. Separately, `/metrics` exposes ten prom-client series (http duration, db pool, ai cost, job runs, stripe webhook failures) and there is no Prometheus/agent configuration in fly.toml, fly.staging.toml, or any workflow — nothing scrapes it. Net: a two-process-group system with 1,725 endpoints, 156 scheduled jobs, and an autonomous decision executor has request logs and Sentry errors, and no spans and no time-series.
- **Fix:** Decide one way and delete the other. Either install the four OTel packages, pin them, point `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` at a backend, and add a boot assertion that fails loudly when `OTEL_EXPORTER` is set but the SDK did not load; or delete server/tracing.ts and lean on Sentry performance tracing, which is already a real dependency. Do not leave a module whose only observable behaviour is a warning. For metrics, either add a scrape (Fly + Grafana Cloud agent) or delete the endpoint.
- **Comparable:** Honeycomb and Datadog both make the SDK a hard dependency and fail the boot when export is configured but unavailable, precisely so this state cannot exist. Sentry's own tracing SDK (already installed here) would give distributed traces for near-zero incremental work.
- **Evidence:** Read server/tracing.ts:1-120; probed node_modules/@opentelemetry for each required module (instrumentation-http, instrumentation-express, exporter-trace-otlp-http all MISSING); read the installed @opentelemetry/resources version (2.6.1); grepped package.json (0 opentelemetry deps); read server/metrics.ts's ten metric definitions and grepped fly.toml + all workflows for a scraper (0 hits).

#### [high/M] Half the client bypasses the fetch wrapper that handles session expiry and mutation idempotency

- **Where:** `client/src/lib/queryClient.ts (`apiRequest`, ~line 380); 525 raw `fetch("/api/...")` call sites across client/src`
- **Defect:** `apiRequest` does two things nothing else does: it mints a single idempotency key for mutating methods and reuses it across a retry, and it transparently refreshes the Clerk `__session` cookie on a 401 and retries once. There are 561 `apiRequest(` call sites and 525 raw `fetch("/api/…")` / `fetch(`/api/…`)` call sites — essentially a coin flip per feature. On the raw half, a customer whose session cookie expires mid-task gets a hard 401 and whatever ad-hoc error handling that call site wrote, instead of a silent refresh; and a retried mutation is a duplicate effect rather than a deduped one. Because it is a convention rather than a boundary, every new component is a fresh coin flip, and the split will keep widening across the next two years.
- **Fix:** Make the wrapper the only way to reach `/api`. Add an ESLint rule (the repo already has a custom-rule harness at eslint-rules/index.cjs — `use-mutation-must-invalidate.cjs` is the same shape) banning `fetch()` with an `/api` literal in client/src, ratcheted to the current 525 and driven down. Codemod the mechanical cases; the repo has done this before (scripts/codemod-client-logger.mjs).
- **Comparable:** Linear and Notion expose exactly one typed client and lint the raw primitive out of the app layer; tRPC/openapi-typescript make it structurally impossible. The specific pattern worth stealing is the ESLint `no-restricted-syntax` ban on the raw primitive, ratcheted rather than big-banged.
- **Evidence:** Read the `apiRequest` implementation in client/src/lib/queryClient.ts including the 401-refresh-and-retry block and the idempotency-key hoist; counted raw `/api` fetches (525) and `apiRequest(` calls (561) across client/src.

#### [high/M] One number, two owners: the autopilot's customer-health guardrail and the founder's customer-health screen use disjoint formulas

- **Where:** `server/services/customerHealthEngine.ts (run at server/jobs/runScheduledJobs.ts:1556, writes at :244) vs server/services/customerHealthScoring.ts (read at server/routes-founder-intelligence.ts:3928, :3949)`
- **Defect:** Two independent 0-100 "customer health" implementations, both live, with disjoint inputs. The engine scores from `lifecycleEvents`, `npsMicroSurveys`, `paxNudges`, and per-module record counts (five weighted components, four bands) and inserts a row per org per cycle into `customer_health_scores`. That table is read by exactly two consumers — server/services/autonomousDecisionExecutor.ts:863 and server/services/agentRetractCron.ts:80 — both of which average it over the last 24h as a *safety signal governing autonomous action*. Meanwhile `customerHealthScoring.ts` scores from `supportTickets`, `churnRiskScores`, `deals` and `leads` with a different formula, and that is what the founder's Customers surface renders and what growthAutomation.ts:513 acts on daily. So the number the autopilot uses to decide whether to keep acting and the number the founder looks at to judge the same customer are computed from non-overlapping evidence and can move in opposite directions. This is the canonical-law-8 violation the repo's own table-count ratchet note articulates ("duplicating any of them would give one number two owners"). Secondary: the engine's write is a plain `insert`, never an upsert, with no retention path — the table grows monotonically forever.
- **Fix:** Pick one definition, make it the projection, and delete the other — then point all four consumers (executor, retract cron, founder route, growth automation) at it. If both axes are genuinely wanted, rename them so they cannot be mistaken for the same quantity (`engagement_health` vs `commercial_health`) and say on the founder screen which one the autopilot is gated on. Add the retention sweep.
- **Comparable:** Any metrics-layer discipline (dbt semantic layer, Cube, Looker's LookML) exists to make one metric have one definition. The specific practice to steal: a metric registry where a second implementation of a named metric is a build failure.
- **Evidence:** Read both files' headers and their query lines (engine: lifecycleEvents/npsMicroSurveys/paxNudges/leads/properties/deals/notes; scoring: supportTickets/deals/leads/churnRiskScores); traced every caller of each module and every reader of `customerHealthScores`; read `runHealthForAllOrgs` at customerHealthEngine.ts:227-258.

#### [high/L] Four independent job systems; the one that fixed the double-charge bug has ~8 callers, the one that caused it has 156

- **Where:** `server/jobs/runScheduledJobs.ts (5,706 lines, 156 `withJobLock`, 105 `start*Job`); server/jobs/scheduler.ts:1-45; server/services/jobQueue.ts (BullMQ or in-memory); the `outbox` table drained by server/worker.ts`
- **Defect:** server/jobs/scheduler.ts's header states plainly why it exists: the legacy `setInterval` pattern caused "concurrent overlapping runs of the same job (e.g. dunning sweeper double-charging customers when the previous run hadn't finished)", silent error loss, and no backoff. `scheduleSelfRescheduling` fixes all three — awaits the run before scheduling the next, persists `job_runs` rows, dead-letters to `outbox_dlq`, exponential backoff. It has roughly eight call sites. The 156 `withJobLock` jobs in runScheduledJobs.ts still run on `trackInterval`/setInterval with a Postgres advisory-style lock, sampled success logging (one row per job per hour) and fire-and-forget failure inserts. Alongside those sit a BullMQ-or-in-memory queue that logs "Jobs will not survive server restarts" when REDIS_URL is unset, and the `outbox` table. Four retry semantics, four failure-visibility stories, four places a new engineer must learn before adding one scheduled task. The line-count ratchet drives runScheduledJobs.ts down ~1% per extraction; at that rate this file outlives the two-year horizon.
- **Fix:** Declare `scheduleSelfRescheduling` the one runtime, and make jobRegistry.ts (which already enumerates all ~116 jobs with cadences and criticality) the thing that *drives* registration rather than merely describing it — the roster already carries an unused `cron` field for exactly this. Extract job bodies into `server/jobs/<group>.ts` modules with a uniform signature and let the registry start them. That converts the line-count ratchet from a slow trim into a mechanical migration and gives every job the same DLQ and backoff.
- **Comparable:** Temporal and Inngest both exist because exactly this sprawl is the default outcome. The cheap version of their lesson: one durable runtime, one retry policy, one registry, and registration derived from the registry so a job cannot exist outside it.
- **Evidence:** Read server/jobs/scheduler.ts:1-45 (the stated failure history), server/utils/jobRuntime.ts in full (trackInterval + withJobLock + sampled logging), server/jobs/jobRegistry.ts:1-60; counted 156 `withJobLock(` in runScheduledJobs.ts and 8 importers of scheduler.ts; read the BullMQ fallback warning.

#### [medium/S] The production job topology is not expressible in the repository

- **Where:** `server/index.ts:792; server/jobs/runScheduledJobs.ts:4; fly.toml `[env]` (lines 26-36)`
- **Defect:** server/index.ts:792 gates the app process on `DISABLE_BACKGROUND_JOBS === "1"` with the comment "they exhaust the DB pool on small instances when run on the customer-facing app machines", and runScheduledJobs.ts:4 asserts as fact that "Production runs the app process group with DISABLE_BACKGROUND_JOBS=1". That variable appears in no fly.toml, no fly.staging.toml, and no workflow — its only occurrence in checked-in config is docker-compose.test.yml:46. So what the repository actually specifies is that the 2-vCPU / 1 GB app machine (`DB_POOL_MAX` default 5, server/db.ts:78) runs all 156 scheduled jobs alongside customer traffic — the precise condition the code says exhausts the pool. Either it is set as an out-of-band Fly secret (in which case a load-bearing topology decision exists only in someone's shell history and cannot be reviewed, diffed, or restored) or it is not set at all.
- **Fix:** Move it into fly.toml as a per-process-group env (Fly supports `[[env]]` scoped by process group, or set it explicitly on the app group), and add a boot assertion: if `process.env.FLY_PROCESS_GROUP === 'app'` and background jobs are enabled, log an explicit warning naming the pool size. Configuration that the code's own comments call load-bearing belongs in the repo.
- **Comparable:** Anything with declarative infra (Terraform, Fly's own `fly.toml` process-group env, Kubernetes manifests) treats runtime env as reviewable code. The pattern worth stealing is a boot-time invariant that refuses to start in a topology the code says is unsafe.
- **Evidence:** Grepped the whole tree for DISABLE_BACKGROUND_JOBS (hits only in server/index.ts, runScheduledJobs.ts, worker.ts, docker-compose.test.yml, docs, and the built dist bundles); read fly.toml's complete `[env]` and `[processes]` blocks and server/db.ts:76-80. HYPOTHESIS: that it is set as a Fly secret — I cannot see Fly state from here; the finding is that the repo cannot express or verify it.

#### [medium/L] Two parallel external-data orchestration layers, contradicting the constitution's own statement

- **Where:** `server/services/data-source-broker.ts (2,491 lines) vs server/services/providers/provider-registry.ts; CLAUDE.md line 48`
- **Defect:** CLAUDE.md states "All external data flows through the provider registry (server/services/providers/)" and describes its tier filtering, credit deduction, circuit breaking and `provider_cache` caching. In fact `data-source-broker.ts` is a complete second implementation of the same four responsibilities — its own `AccessTier` union, its own 30-value `LookupCategory` union, `TIER_PRIORITY` from dataSourceOrdering.ts, `enrichmentCircuitBreaker`/`countyApiCircuitBreaker` from server/utils/circuitBreaker.ts, and a `data_source_cache` table — with roughly twenty callers including server/ai/tools.ts and server/ai/supportAgent.ts (the two model-driven tool surfaces), acquisitionRadar, propertyEnrichment and leadScoring. The registry has roughly ten. There are eight distinct circuit-breaker implementations in server/. A new engineer asked to add a data vendor has two plausible homes, two cache tables, two breaker stores, and a CLAUDE.md that names only one of them.
- **Fix:** Either fold the broker's category set and ordering into the registry (the registry already has the richer license/BYOK/change-detection machinery) and leave the broker as a thin adapter, or amend CLAUDE.md to say honestly that there are two planes and which one a new vendor belongs in. The second is a half-day and stops the bleeding; the first is the real fix.
- **Comparable:** Stripe's single `Connector` abstraction and Segment's destination registry both enforce one plane per integration category, with the registry as the only construction site. The important half is that the docs and the code cannot disagree because registration is the doc.
- **Evidence:** Read the head of both modules including their tier/category/breaker/cache imports; enumerated callers of each (~20 vs ~10); grepped the four cache tables in shared/schema.ts (provider_cache:3707, cached_lookups:3781, data_source_cache:9178) and eight files carrying a circuit-breaker implementation.

#### [medium/L] 273 route modules and 1,725 handlers hand-mounted in a 2,721-line file, with ordering as an unmodelled hazard

- **Where:** `server/routes.ts (261 imports, 141 `app.use()` mounts, 164 `register*Routes()` calls, 33 inline handlers); server/routeManifest.ts:11-12; scripts/check-route-order.mjs:16-20`
- **Defect:** Mounting is a manual, order-sensitive, 2,721-line procedure. routeManifest.ts explicitly forbids wiring off itself ("DO NOT wire mounting off this manifest — that would reorder middleware"), so the manifest is a descriptive mirror that can only detect drift after the fact, never prevent it. Meanwhile check-route-order.mjs states "cross-file ordering isn't modeled (registration order across files isn't derivable statically)" — but with 141 mounts and 164 register-calls interleaved in one function, cross-file order is exactly where the risk now lives; the same-file check that found 28 live shadowing bugs in 2026-07 cannot see it. Route files also range to 113 handlers in one file (routes-founder-intelligence.ts, 4,456 lines). A new engineer adding an endpoint must know which of 273 files it belongs in, whether it is router- or register-style, where in the 2,721 lines its mount must sit relative to auth/rate-limit/white-label middleware, and to update the manifest in the same commit.
- **Fix:** Make mounting derived. Group middleware into explicit, ordered *phases* (public → auth → org-scoped → founder → catch-all) and have routes.ts iterate the manifest within each phase, so the manifest becomes the source of truth rather than a mirror and cross-file order becomes a declared property a test can assert. That also makes check-route-order's cross-file blind spot closeable.
- **Comparable:** NestJS modules, Fastify plugin encapsulation, and Rails engines all make mount order a declared property of a module rather than a line position in one file. File-system routing (Next.js, Remix) is the other well-trodden answer and removes the manifest entirely.
- **Evidence:** Counted imports/mounts/register-calls/inline handlers in server/routes.ts; read routeManifest.ts:1-46 including the DO-NOT-WIRE instruction; read check-route-order.mjs:1-21; counted 1,725 handlers across server/ and the per-file top ten.

#### [medium/L] 17% of the schema is dead and roughly 400 service exports are unreachable — frozen, but with no paydown path

- **Where:** `scripts/ratchets/reachability.json (`baselines`); shared/schema.ts (464 pgTables) + shared/schema/*.ts (260)`
- **Defect:** The reachability ratchet — an excellent gate — currently pins: 57 tables with no writer, 67 tables with no reader (124 of 728, or 17% of the schema), 373 unreached exports across server/services and server/jobs, 1,171 exported-but-module-private symbols, 28 module orphans, 4 unregistered route files, plus 54/427/1 more in shared. Every one of those is loaded on boot, type-checked on every `npm run check`, and read by every engineer trying to understand a subsystem. The ratchet guarantees the number does not grow; nothing schedules it down, and the table-count ratchet's own north star ("<=450 tables") is 278 tables away. Two years of this trajectory is a codebase where the majority of what a new engineer reads is inert.
- **Fix:** Give the ratchet a budget, not just a ceiling: a standing rule that each merged wave lowers one reachability family by at least its own additions plus N. Start with `tablesNoReader` (67) — a table nothing reads is a black hole that accumulates tenant rows forever and is also a data-retention liability. The deletion-ledger discipline already exists and worked (13 tables dropped 2026-08-16); it needs a cadence.
- **Comparable:** Google's and Meta's large monorepos run automated dead-code deletion bots against exactly this signal. The pattern worth stealing is that the finding auto-opens a deletion PR rather than incrementing a counter.
- **Evidence:** Read scripts/ratchets/reachability.json's baselines object and the lint's header (scripts/lint-reachability.mjs:1-60, including the eight named historical instances); counted 464 pgTables in shared/schema.ts and 260 in shared/schema/*.ts.

#### [medium/S] The terminal error handler breaks the documented error contract precisely where the request id matters most

- **Where:** `server/index.ts:741-753; server/utils/errors.ts:6-26; scripts/ratchets/res-status-raw.json (baseline 498)`
- **Defect:** CLAUDE.md and server/utils/errors.ts define the API error shape as `{ error, message, details?, statusCode, docsUrl?, requestId? }`, and errors.ts goes to real trouble to stamp `requestId` from the correlation-id middleware on 5xx "so users can paste it into support". The Express terminal handler at server/index.ts:747-753 emits `res.status(status).json({ message })` — no `error` code, no `statusCode`, no `requestId`, and in production the message is flattened to "Internal Server Error". So the one case where a user most needs a correlation id (an unhandled throw out of any of 1,725 handlers) is the one case that has none, and the client cannot distinguish it from a handled 500. Compounding this, 498 raw `res.status(X).json(...)` sites are frozen in the ratchet against a constitution that says "Always use `Errors.*`" — the canonical helper has roughly a third as many call sites as its competitor.
- **Fix:** Route the terminal handler through `Errors.internal(res, err)` so there is exactly one 5xx producer. That is a five-line change and immediately gives every uncaught exception a request id, a code, and Sentry capture. Then drive the res-status-raw baseline down with a codemod on the mechanical cases.
- **Comparable:** Stripe's error envelope is uniform down to the last handler and always carries `request_id`; that is the single thing that makes their support loop work. The pattern to steal is one and only one function that can produce a 5xx body.
