Prefix invalidation on `QK.leads.list` (`["/api/leads"]`) reaches the first and can never reach the second, so `RELATED.lead` has a hole by construction. The same split runs through the app: 940 single-string keys vs 329 segmented keys vs 58 interpolated. `QK.notes.forEntity` (`["/api/notes", { entityType, entityId }]`) would additionally produce `/api/notes/[object Object]` under the default `queryKey.join("/")` fetcher (queryClient.ts getQueryFn).
- **Fix:** Pick ONE key shape — segmented `[resource, ...params]` is the only one prefix-invalidation works on — and make the registry the sole producer. Then get real adoption before adding entries: migrate the leads/deals/properties/tasks/dashboard hooks first, fix QK.leads.sms and QK.notes.forEntity to the segmented shape, and promote `acreos/use-mutation-must-invalidate` from warn to a rule that requires `invalidateRelated` or a `// allow-no-invalidation:` reason. Add a lint that fails on a `queryKey` literal whose first element contains a `/` after the resource segment, so the string form cannot come back.
- **Evidence:** Read client/src/lib/query-keys.ts in full. `grep -rl "from \"@/lib/query-keys\"" client/src` → 0 files; `grep -rn "invalidateRelated" client/src` → 4 hits, all inside query-keys.ts. Programmatic count over client/src: 940 single-string / 329 segmented / 58 interpolated queryKey literals; 668 invalidateQueries call sites; 1,327 `queryKey: [` literals.

#### [medium/S] A regex HTML sanitizer with zero callers and a docstring inviting its use sits next to the DOMPurify the app actually ships

- **Where:** `client/src/lib/sanitize.ts:1-33`
- **Defect:** sanitize.ts exports `sanitizeHtml()` — a four-regex 'sanitizer' whose docblock says `Usage: <div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: sanitizeHtml(html) }} />`. It has zero importers. It is also trivially bypassable: EVENT_HANDLERS is `/\s+on\w+\s*=\s*["'][^"']*["']/gi`, so it requires quoted values and `<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>` passes through untouched; BLOCKED_TAGS lists script/iframe/object/embed/link/style/meta/base/form but not `img`, `svg` or `math`, so `<svg onload=…>` also passes; and nested-tag tricks (`<scr<script>ipt>`) survive a single-pass replace. Meanwhile isomorphic-dompurify is already a dependency and is the thing actually used at all seven real render sites (inbox.tsx:592, offers.tsx:476, field-note-detail.tsx:110, native-mail-panel.tsx:321, buyer-blasts.tsx:267, documents.tsx:1194, deals/AssignmentPanel.tsx:609). The danger is not today's behaviour — it is that the next engineer who needs to render HTML will find the file whose name and docstring say 'use me' and ship an XSS on rendered counterparty email.
- **Fix:** Delete client/src/lib/sanitize.ts. If a shared wrapper is wanted, re-export a configured DOMPurify from that path so the obvious import name resolves to the safe implementation.
- **Evidence:** Read sanitize.ts in full and evaluated its four regexes by inspection. `grep -rn "sanitizeHtml|from \"@/lib/sanitize\"" client/src` excluding the file itself → 0 hits. Enumerated all 11 dangerouslySetInnerHTML sites and confirmed the seven user-content ones use DOMPurify.sanitize while the rest are static JSON-LD or the shadcn chart style tag.

#### [medium/S] A stale eslint ignore removes page-shell.tsx — used by 190 files — from every custom lint rule

- **Where:** `eslint.config.js:47-51`
- **Defect:** The active flat config ignores `client/src/components/page-shell.tsx` with the comment 'Pre-existing JSX parse error — a comment was placed at a position where the JSX grammar doesn't accept it. Out of scope for the persona-leak codemod; un-ignore once that lands.' That parse error no longer exists: running `npx eslint client/src/components/page-shell.tsx --no-ignore --format json` returns `errorCount: 0, fatalErrorCount: 0, messages: []`. So the app-wide shell — imported by 190 files, and the component that renders the Sidebar, the topbar, the main landmark and both banner slots — is permanently exempt from acreos/icon-button-needs-aria-label (an ERROR rule), no-founder-codenames-in-customer-jsx (an ERROR rule), no-hardcoded-color-literals and prefer-verbs-canon. The eslint ratchet's MIN_LINTED_FILES floor is designed to catch a collapsed population, and a single-file exemption slips under it.
- **Fix:** Delete lines 47-51 of eslint.config.js and re-measure scripts/eslint-rules-baseline.json in the same commit. Then add a guard to lint-eslint-ratchet.mjs that fails if the `ignores` array grows anything under `client/src/**` — an exemption should be a deliberate, reviewed change, not a comment that outlives its reason.
- **Evidence:** Read eslint.config.js:38-51. Ran `npx eslint client/src/components/page-shell.tsx --no-ignore --format json` and got zero messages and zero fatal errors — the stated reason for the ignore is no longer true. `grep -rl PageShell client/src --include=*.tsx` → 190 files.

#### [medium/M] Zero React.memo in 268k LOC, the only virtualization primitive has zero consumers, and a dead component drags recharts

- **Where:** `client/src (0 memo call sites), client/src/components/VirtualTable.tsx (0 consumers), client/src/components/stat-card.tsx:4 (0 consumers, imports recharts)`
- **Defect:** Three related symptoms of the same gap. (1) `React.memo`/`memo(` appears zero times in client/src — with 152 useMemo and 142 useCallback, the team memoizes values but never components, so every parent re-render walks the whole child tree. On the god components below that is the difference between a 2ms and a 40ms keystroke. (2) `VirtualTable.tsx` wraps @tanstack/react-virtual (a shipped dependency, package.json:151) and has zero importers — so the one row-windowing primitive in the repo is dead while list pages render every row. (3) `stat-card.tsx` has zero importers and imports `{ AreaChart, Area, ResponsiveContainer, Tooltip }` from recharts; three separate local `StatCard` implementations exist instead (portfolio-pnl.tsx:78, founder-providers.tsx:187, and the skeleton in list-skeleton.tsx:85).
- **Fix:** Delete stat-card.tsx and VirtualTable.tsx, or adopt VirtualTable on the leads/properties/deals tables and keep it. Then memoize the row components of the paginated tables (leads.tsx:1630 and :1829 render `paginatedLeads.map`) so filter/selection state changes don't re-render 25 rows of Radix menus. Prioritise by measurement, not by grep count — profile the leads table with 25 rows and a selection toggle first.
- **Comparable:** Linear's issue list and Notion's database views window rows and memoize the row component; both stay smooth at 10k rows. AcreOS's leads page paginates at 25 server-side, which is the right first defence — but it has no answer for the unpaginated lists (inbox threads, receipts, decision queue).
- **Evidence:** `grep -rn '\bmemo(' client/src` excluding useMemo/useCallback → 0 hits. `grep -rn VirtualTable client/src` excluding its own file → 0 hits. `grep -rn 'from "@/components/stat-card"' client/src` → 0 hits; read stat-card.tsx:4 for the recharts import. Counted useMemo=152, useCallback=142.

#### [medium/S] Four of six context providers allocate a fresh value object every render, and the root re-renders on every navigation

- **Where:** `client/src/contexts/dynamic-island-context.tsx:46, client/src/contexts/feature-flags-context.tsx:73, client/src/contexts/pax-rail-context.tsx:80-84, client/src/contexts/theme-context.tsx:282-291; App.tsx:2229-2232`
- **Defect:** `accessibility-context.tsx:118` and `tenant-theme-context.tsx:142` do this correctly with useMemo. The other four pass an inline object literal — `value={{ message, show, dismiss }}`, `value={{ ...state, refresh }}`, `value={{ isOpen, setOpen, toggle, pendingContext, ... }}`, `value={{ themeConfig, setThemeConfig, resolvedMode, theme, setTheme, toggleTheme }}` — so the context value identity changes on every render of the provider and every consumer re-renders unconditionally. That would be harmless if the providers rarely rendered, but `LocationAwareErrorBoundary` (App.tsx:2229-2232) calls `useLocation()` and wraps the ENTIRE provider stack, so every wouter navigation re-renders the root and therefore all four providers. feature-flags-context is the worst: `refresh` is a plain function redeclared each render and is part of the value. theme-context also declares `setTheme`/`toggleTheme` as fresh closures at :277-279.
- **Fix:** Wrap all four values in useMemo with correct deps and wrap the callbacks in useCallback (the pattern accessibility-context.tsx:118 already uses — copy it). Separately, move the location subscription out of the root: give ErrorBoundary an internal `useLocation` inside a leaf component, or move LocationAwareErrorBoundary inside the Router so a navigation does not re-render the provider stack.
- **Evidence:** Grepped every `value={` in client/src/contexts and read each provider's surrounding lines; confirmed useMemo present in exactly two of six. Read App.tsx:2227-2232 and 2236-2280 to confirm LocationAwareErrorBoundary is the outermost wrapper above ThemeProvider/QueryClientProvider/etc.

#### [medium/S] Three parallel feature-flag systems, one of which fetches on every boot and has no readers

- **Where:** `client/src/contexts/feature-flags-context.tsx (useFlag: 0 consumers), client/src/hooks/use-feature-flags.ts, client/src/lib/featureFlags.ts`
- **Defect:** `contexts/feature-flags-context.tsx` mounts in the root tree (App.tsx:2255), fires `/api/feature-flags` on every authenticated boot (:66-72), builds a `Record<string, boolean>` — and `useFlag()` has ZERO call sites anywhere in client/src. The only thing that consumes the provider is `useFeatureFlagsRefresh` in pages/founder/features.tsx:10, i.e. one founder admin page's refresh button. So every customer pays a boot request for a map nobody reads. Meanwhile `hooks/use-feature-flags.ts` is the real, well-reasoned route-level registry (with the controlledRoutes/deny-list precedence analysis documented at :31-60) used by App.tsx and layout-sidebar.tsx, and `lib/featureFlags.ts` is a third, localStorage-keyed system for the founder-UI toggle. Three systems named almost identically is a live foot-gun: a future engineer reaching for 'the feature flag hook' has a 2-in-3 chance of picking one that does not gate anything.
- **Fix:** Delete `useFlag` and the flags map from feature-flags-context.tsx, reducing it to the refresh trigger the founder page actually needs (and lazy-mount it under /founder so customers never make the request), or fold that refresh into hooks/use-feature-flags.ts and delete the context entirely. Rename lib/featureFlags.ts to something that cannot be confused with it (e.g. lib/founder-ui-toggle.ts).
- **Evidence:** Read all three files. `grep -rn 'useFlag(' client/src` excluding contexts/ → 0 hits; `grep -rn useFeatureFlagsContext client/src` → 0 hits. Only consumer of the context is pages/founder/features.tsx:10 (useFeatureFlagsRefresh). App.tsx:11 and layout-sidebar.tsx:159 import the *hook* version.

#### [medium/L] Form handling is inconsistent: 13 files use react-hook-form + zod, ~65 hand-roll useState validation

- **Where:** `client/src (78 files containing `<form`, 14 containing useForm, 13 using zodResolver)`
- **Defect:** react-hook-form@7.81 and zod@4.4 are dependencies and there is a proper shadcn `ui/form.tsx` wrapper, but only 13 surfaces use `useForm` + `zodResolver` (template-editor, deal-calculator, document-generator, campaigns-content, mail-settings-content, finance, listings, tasks, leads, deals, counties, properties, documents). The other ~65 `<form>` surfaces roll their own controlled state and validation, which in practice means: no shared error rendering, no `aria-invalid`/`aria-describedby` wiring (CLAUDE.md requires every input to have an associated label — the ui/form.tsx path gives you that for free, the ad-hoc path does not), no dirty-state guard on navigate-away, and validation rules duplicated instead of reused from shared zod schemas. It also means the client and server can disagree about what is valid, because the ad-hoc forms are not derived from a shared schema.
- **Fix:** Do not sweep all 65. Establish the rule ('a form that writes to the API uses useForm + zodResolver against a schema exported from shared/'), convert the highest-consequence surfaces first — anything writing money or sending on the customer's behalf (finance drawers, offers.tsx, outreach/mail/compose.tsx) — and add a lint that a `<form onSubmit>` in a file without `useForm` requires an explicit `// allow-manual-form: <reason>` comment, ratcheted at the measured count so it can only shrink.
- **Evidence:** `grep -rln '<form' client/src --include=*.tsx | wc -l` = 78; of those, 77 do not contain `useForm`. `grep -rln useForm client/src` = 14 files (one of which is ui/form.tsx itself); `grep -rln zodResolver client/src` = 13 files.

#### [medium/M] Two hundred and six files bypass apiRequest, losing the 30s timeout and the 401 refresh-retry the repo added on purpose

- **Where:** `client/src (206 files with `await fetch(`), e.g. client/src/components/deal-inbox.tsx:49,65; client/src/components/email-compose-sheet.tsx:81; client/src/components/pax-copilot-rail.tsx:695,757,997`
- **Defect:** `apiRequest` (lib/queryClient.ts:331-395) gives every call three things a raw fetch does not: `AbortSignal.timeout(30_000)` — added, per its own comment, because unbounded fetches were 'the single biggest cause of the indefinite spinner UX symptom in the 2026-05-01 audit'; a transparent Clerk `__session` refresh-and-retry on 401; and `throwIfResNotOk`, which converts a non-2xx into an `ApiError` carrying `status`, the server's `message` and `docsUrl` so the global MutationCache toast can render 'Learn why'. 206 files call `fetch` directly and get none of it. The CSRF header is covered globally by lib/csrf-fetch.ts, so that specific risk is handled — but a raw-fetch mutation on a tab whose session cookie just expired fails with a generic error where apiRequest would have silently refreshed and succeeded, and a stalled request hangs forever with the spinner the timeout exists to kill.
- **Fix:** Codemod the mutating call sites to apiRequest first (they are the ones where a silent 401 costs the user work), then the GET queryFns. Add a lint over client/src that flags `fetch("/api/...")` outside lib/, ratcheted at the measured count — the population must be the whole of client/src, not a sample, per CLAUDE.md's third law.
- **Evidence:** Read lib/queryClient.ts:331-420 (apiRequest, doApiFetch, the 401 retry and the timeout comment) and lib/csrf-fetch.ts in full. `grep -rl 'await fetch(' client/src | wc -l` = 206; a scan for a POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE method within 14 lines of a `fetch(` found 263 mutating call sites.

#### [medium/M] The route Suspense fallback paints one KPI-dashboard silhouette for all 301 routes

- **Where:** `client/src/components/route-fallback.tsx:16-58, used at client/src/App.tsx:712`
- **Defect:** RouteFallback is well built for the page it was designed against — sidebar rail, topbar, a title, a four-tile KPI grid and one large card, with role=status/aria-live/aria-busy and an sr-only 'Loading page…'. But it is the ONLY fallback, and it plays for all 301 routes behind the single Suspense boundary at App.tsx:712. /inbox is a three-pane mail client, /ai is a chat stream, /map is a full-bleed canvas, /settings/pax is a stack of switch rows: none of them look like four KPI tiles and a chart. A mis-shaped skeleton is worse than a neutral one, because the user's eye settles on a layout that then jumps when the real content arrives — the layout shift the doc comment says it exists to prevent.
- **Fix:** Once the shell is hoisted (see the persistent-shell finding), give the content region a small set of shape-matched fallbacks — `dashboard` (current), `list`, `split`, `canvas`, `form` — and let each route pick one, the way Next.js App Router's per-segment loading.tsx does. `page-skeleton.tsx` already has the beginnings of this vocabulary ('table' — stat-card row + table header + rows); wire it into the route boundary instead of leaving it page-local.
- **Comparable:** Next.js App Router per-segment loading.tsx; Linear's per-view skeletons. Both match the destination's geometry, not one global silhouette.
- **Evidence:** Read route-fallback.tsx in full and App.tsx:702-720 confirming one Suspense wraps the entire Switch (`grep -c '<Route ' client/src/App.tsx` = 301). Read page-skeleton.tsx:8 for the existing shape vocabulary.

#### [medium/S] The Pax ask expiry countdown never ticks, so Approve stays enabled on an ask that has already expired

- **Where:** `client/src/components/pax/PaxAskCard.tsx:73-88 (expiresInWords), :141 (computed once per render), :182-190 (rendered), :211 (showButtons gate)`
- **Defect:** `expiresInWords(ask.expiresAt)` takes `now = Date.now()` as a default argument and is evaluated once per render at line 141. Nothing schedules a re-render, so on a card sitting open in the decision queue the label freezes at whatever it said when the list last refetched. The queue's own poll (usePaxNeedsYou.ts:121, POLL_FALLBACK_MS) will eventually correct it, but between polls a user can read 'expires in 2m', watch it not change, tap Approve after it has actually lapsed, and get a server rejection — on the one surface in the product whose entire job is making a consequential send legible before you tap. `status` is derived only from `ask.status === "expired" || ask.expired`, i.e. from server state, never from the wall clock the card is already displaying.
- **Fix:** Add a one-second (or, for long horizons, one-minute) interval in PaxAskCard that bumps a `now` state used by both `expiresInWords(ask.expiresAt, now)` and the `status` derivation, so the card flips itself to `expired` — disabling Approve/Reject and offering 'Ask Pax to draft it again' — the moment the deadline passes, without waiting for a poll. Keep the interval scoped to cards that actually have an `expiresAt` and are still `pending`.
- **Comparable:** Gmail's undo-send and Stripe Radar's review queue both tick their deadline live and disable the action at zero rather than letting you tap into a server rejection.
- **Evidence:** Read PaxAskCard.tsx end to end. Line 141 `const expiresIn = expiresInWords(ask.expiresAt);` with no dependency on any state that changes over time; line 133 derives `status` from server fields only; no setInterval/setTimeout anywhere in the file. This is a product-quality gap in the wave-1 surface, not a wave-1 correctness bug — the spec's §8 deferrals do not cover it.

#### [medium/S] NotificationBanner's auto-hide timer is never cleared: back-to-back notifications truncate each other and the timer outlives unmount

- **Where:** `client/src/components/notification-banner.tsx:36-60 (handleEvent), specifically the setTimeout at :53-56`
- **Defect:** `handleEvent` calls `setTimeout(() => setShowBanner(false), hideDelay)` with no handle stored and no cleanup. Two consequences. (1) If a second notification arrives 1s after the first, the first notification's timer is still pending and fires 4s into the second one's display — so the second banner disappears after 4s instead of 5, and a burst of notifications gets progressively shorter until the last one flashes. Because this component listens on `founder:activity`, which the double-dispatch bug above already fires twice per event, this is reachable on a single notification. (2) The timer holds a closure over `setShowBanner` past unmount — NotificationBanner is lazily mounted and conditionally rendered on `user` (App.tsx:2206), so it does unmount on sign-out.
- **Fix:** Store the handle in a ref, `clearTimeout` the previous one at the top of handleEvent, and clear it in a `useEffect(() => () => clearTimeout(ref.current), [])`. Better: drive the auto-hide from a `useEffect` keyed on `latestNotif.id` so the timer is owned by the render that shows the banner rather than by the event handler.
- **Evidence:** Read notification-banner.tsx:30-80. No `clearTimeout` appears anywhere in the file; the timeout is created inside a useCallback with `[]` deps.

#### [medium/S] WebSocket reconnect has no jitter, no attempt cap and no visibility/online gating, on module-global state shared by every hook instance

- **Where:** `client/src/hooks/use-realtime.ts:28-31 (module globals), :97-107 (onclose backoff), :108-110 (onerror)`
- **Defect:** `reconnectDelay` and `reconnectTimer` are module-level singletons, but every `useRealtime()` instance can write them. `ws.onclose` schedules a timer without first clearing an existing one, so two sockets closing together (which the CONNECTING race above makes likely) can leave orphaned timers each driving their own `connect()`. The backoff itself is `Math.min(reconnectDelay * 2, 30_000)` with no jitter — every client of a restarting server retries in lockstep, which is exactly the thundering herd a deploy produces. There is no attempt cap and no gating on `document.visibilityState` or `navigator.onLine`, so a backgrounded tab with no network reconnects forever at 30s intervals; note the query defaults deliberately set `refetchIntervalInBackground: false` for precisely this reason (queryClient.ts) — the socket layer never got the same treatment. Finally `ws.onerror` calls `ws.close()`, which means a rejected handshake (e.g. auth failure) loops indefinitely rather than surfacing.
- **Fix:** Fold this into the singleton-store refactor: one owner of `reconnectTimer` that clears before scheduling, full-jitter backoff (`random() * min(cap, base * 2^n)`), a pause when `document.hidden` or `!navigator.onLine` with an immediate reconnect on `online`/`visibilitychange`, and a distinguishable terminal state for handshake rejection so the UI can say 'live updates unavailable' instead of pretending.
- **Comparable:** AWS's published full-jitter algorithm is the reference; Pusher, Ably and Linear's client all combine jittered backoff with visibility gating so a laptop lid closing does not generate reconnect traffic.
- **Evidence:** Read use-realtime.ts:28-121 in full. Confirmed: no jitter, no clearTimeout before scheduling in onclose, no visibility or online checks, no attempt cap.

#### [medium/S] Global refetchOnWindowFocus:false means a long-open tab shows indefinitely stale money and pipeline numbers

- **Where:** `client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:530-534 (defaultOptions.queries), 7 opt-ins across client/src`
- **Defect:** The query defaults are `refetchOnWindowFocus: false`, `refetchInterval: false`, `staleTime: 2min`, `gcTime: 30min`. Because navigation remounts pages (see the shell finding), route changes do refetch — but a user who stays on /finance, /portfolio or a note detail and switches to another tab for two hours comes back to two-hour-old numbers with nothing on screen saying so. Only 7 queries in the whole client opt back in with `refetchOnWindowFocus: true`. For a category of software where the numbers are the product, silently-stale is the wrong default: the app looks confidently wrong rather than honestly loading.
- **Fix:** Flip the default to `refetchOnWindowFocus: true` (staleTime already prevents a refetch storm for anything fetched in the last 2 minutes, so the cost is one request per genuinely-stale query on focus), and opt OUT explicitly on the handful of expensive or deliberately-frozen queries. If the founder prefers the current default, the honest alternative is a visible 'as of HH:MM' stamp on money surfaces — but do not leave it silent.
- **Comparable:** Linear, Superhuman and Stripe Dashboard all refetch on focus; Stripe additionally stamps 'Updated just now' on financial figures so staleness is never invisible.
- **Evidence:** Read queryClient.ts defaultOptions (lines with refetchInterval:false, refetchIntervalInBackground:false, refetchOnWindowFocus:false, staleTime STALE_TIMES.medium = 2min, gcTime CACHE_TIMES.medium = 30min). `grep -rn 'refetchOnWindowFocus: true' client/src | wc -l` = 7.

#### [medium/L] Five god components: 2,499 / 1,601 / 1,552 / 1,549 / 1,313 lines in a single function body

- **Where:** `client/src/components/property-map.tsx:868-3367 (PropertyMap, 2,499 lines); client/src/pages/borrower-portal.tsx:446-2047 (BorrowerDashboard, 1,601); client/src/components/pax-copilot-rail.tsx:333-1884 (PaxCopilotRail, 1,552); client/src/pages/leads.tsx:680-2229 (LeadsPageDesktop, 1,549); client/src/components/command-palette.tsx:286-1598 (CommandPalette, 1,313)`
- **Defect:** These are the five files to refactor first, and the number that matters is not the file length but the single-function length. `PropertyMap` is one 2,499-line component holding 19 useState and 22 useEffect — the effect dependency arrays at property-map.tsx:1364 and :1212 already list four and three memoized layer functions each, which is the signature of a component that has outgrown its own dependency graph, and with `react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` disabled (see the first finding) nothing checks that any of those 22 arrays are correct. `CommandPalette` is the sole component in its file — no seams at all. `PaxCopilotRail` is the customer's primary conversational surface and mounts on every non-/ai, non-/founder page (App.tsx:2201), so its render cost is paid app-wide. All five are unmemoized (zero React.memo in the client), so any state change re-renders the entire body.
- **Fix:** Refactor by extracting hooks before extracting JSX — that is where the risk lives. For PropertyMap: pull each layer family into its own `useXLayer(map)` hook (FEMA flood, zoning, ArcGIS/WMS overlays, comps, nearby parcels, measurement, solar) so each owns its own effect and cleanup, then the component becomes a composition of ~8 hooks plus a toolbar. For CommandPalette: extract one `useCommandGroups()` returning the group list, and render groups from data. For LeadsPageDesktop: the table, the filter bar and the import flow are three independent components sharing one server-pagination hook. Do PropertyMap and CommandPalette first — they have the cleanest seams. Do not touch PaxAskCard or the queryClient while doing this.
- **Evidence:** Measured each file's component spans programmatically by locating top-level component declarations and taking the distance to the next one. Verified counts in property-map.tsx: 19 `useState(`, 22 `useEffect(`; read its effect dependency arrays at lines 999-1649. Confirmed CommandPalette is the only component in command-palette.tsx (1 declaration found in 1,598 lines).

## Principal Backend Engineer — request lifecycle, tenant isolation, data access, transactions, locking, idempotency, error taxonomy

**The canonical rules in this backend are genuinely elite; their ADOPTION is partial in exactly the way CLAUDE.md's three laws predict — the CF-edge-IP fix landed on two rate limiters and not the other four, the tenancy lint reads two directories and not the eight where routes live, the reachability gate counts a write inside a module nothing imports as "this table has a writer" — and three of those gaps are live in production today.**


### Already at or above the bar — do not "fix" these

- server/utils/errors.ts is better than most funded SaaS backends. Thirteen semantically distinct helpers with genuinely different meanings (featureUnavailable vs notFound vs notImplemented vs unprocessable vs paymentRequired vs legalHoldActive vs badGateway vs gatewayTimeout), a KB deep-link per error, a requestId surfaced only on 5xx, production message suppression, and `resolveRequestId` defensively probing `res.getHeader` so the error reporter cannot itself throw. Do not touch this file except to route the terminal handler through it.
- The Lob webhook (server/routes/lob-webhooks.ts:96-260) is idempotent BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by a claim table: HMAC-SHA256 over `${timestamp}.${rawBody}` with timingSafeEqual and a staleness window, a monotone STATUS_RANK that refuses to move a piece backwards, and `COALESCE(existing, new)` write-once timestamps so a replay cannot overwrite a real scan time. This is the best webhook handler in the repo and should be the template for the others.
- ACH autopay's safety does NOT depend on the job lock. achAutopayRun.ts:11-16 states that submission is at-most-once per (note, period) because of a unique claim, with the lock as an optimisation. That is the correct layering — idempotency in the database, coordination in the lock — and it is why the withJobLock lease defect is not already a money incident.
- The approval kernel's core contract (server/services/approvalKernel.ts) is right: canonicalised args, a sha256 content hash re-verified at approval time so tampering after the human saw the draft is refused, a guarded pending→approved UPDATE so exactly one tap wins and the loser reads the winner's result, and an append-only pax_sends audit with no UPDATE path. Fix the stranded-'approved' sweep; leave the design alone.
- Tenant resolution re-verifies membership on EVERY request. getOrCreateOrg.ts:83-113 will not honour the active-org cookie unless the caller still owns the org or holds an active team_members row — a revoked seat cannot ride a stale cookie. And because the active org IS a cookie, httpCacheHeaders' `Vary: Cookie` correctly prevents a post-switch cached body from the previous org, which is a subtle trap most teams fall into.
- storage/listCap.ts's LOUD truncation cap. Refusing to return unbounded result sets, and logging when truncation happens instead of silently slicing, is the right instinct — the Today finding is about the caller demanding too much, not about this guardrail.
- supportAgent's `query_org_data` tool (server/ai/supportAgent.ts:4060-4185): every branch carries `eq(table.organizationId, org.id)`, `by_id` scopes org AND id, and the one `sql.raw` is confined to a hardcoded per-entity field whitelist with the search term parameterised. This is the correct shape for a model-driven data tool and should be the template for every other agent tool file.
- The ratchet infrastructure's vacuity floors. Every scripts/ratchets/*.json carries a `minima` on the SCAN POPULATION with an explicit note — "a scan that stops seeing files finds zero and reports it as progress" — plus stale-baseline detection that fails when a count drops without being locked in. Almost no team thinks of this. The route-shadowing gate at baseline 0 with five separate population floors (including the predicate's left- and right-hand sides independently) is exemplary.
- Boot-time DDL was removed in favour of a Fly release_command (documented at server/index.ts:527-534), and the pool is deliberately sized for pgBouncer transaction mode with a 30s statement_timeout and an explicit warning against app-layer prepared statements (server/db.ts:7-23). Both are correct calls that a less careful team gets wrong.
- The rate-limiter family in server/middleware/rateLimit.ts is the CORRECT one — getClientIp everywhere, Redis-backed shared budgets, composite email/user/org keys with a documented CGNAT rationale in memory/feedback_rate_limit_ip_keying.md. The critical limiter finding is entirely about the older inline family in index.ts that shadows it; do not 'fix' this file.

### Benchmarked against

- Stripe — Idempotency-Key with 24h response replay on every mutating request; two-phase webhook processing (record the event id AND mark completion) so a crashed consumer is redelivered rather than silently skipped. AcreOS has neither, and both are the direct fix for the requestTimeout duplicate-write and the Stripe claim-crash findings.
- Clerk / WorkOS Organizations / Auth0 Organizations — org_id and org_role carried in the verified session JWT so tenant resolution costs zero database round-trips. AcreOS is already on Clerk and instead pays 2-6 unindexed queries per request through getOrCreateOrg + hydrateUser, doubled by the /api catch-all.
- Supabase / Neon RLS (`organization_id = current_setting('app.org_id')` forced at the session) — makes the instant-deal-hunt leak structurally impossible instead of review-dependent. AcreOS's `forOrg()` in server/utils/orgScopedDb.ts is the same idea at the app layer; the gap is that ~280 route files never adopted it and the lint never looks there.
- Temporal / Sidekiq Enterprise / Postgres advisory locks — leases that are heartbeated, or session-scoped locks with no TTL to outrun, instead of "TTL slightly less than the interval", which guarantees the lock expires mid-run on any slow job.
- Linear / Superhuman / GitHub notifications — the ranked inbox slice is computed in the database with a covering index and a LIMIT. None of them loads the workspace into app memory to pick three rows, which is what GET /api/today does with up to 20,000 wide rows.
- Cloudflare Rate Limiting / Fastly — key on the true client identity (CF-Connecting-IP) or the authenticated principal, never on the network path. AcreOS wrote this analysis itself in server/utils/clientIp.ts and then applied it to two of six limiters.
- Rails `protect_from_forgery` + Turbo / Django's CSRF failure view — a CSRF rejection carries a distinguishable code and the client transparently re-issues and retries, instead of a raw 403 that reads to the user as "Save did nothing".
- Modern Treasury / Stripe balance transactions — ledger mutations are single relative statements or run under SELECT … FOR UPDATE inside a transaction; a JSONB audit array rebuilt in application memory is the row most likely to be lost.
- Shopify / GitHub API — every error, 500s included, returns a stable machine-readable code plus an X-Request-Id that support asks for first. AcreOS built exactly this in server/utils/errors.ts and then omitted it from the terminal handler that catches the unanticipated failures.
- pganalyze / AWS Performance Insights — flag unindexed foreign keys as a first-class finding precisely because Postgres does not auto-index FK columns and parent deletes then scan every child table. 109 org-scoped tables here are in that state.

### Findings


#### [critical/S] MOST LIKELY 90-DAY INCIDENT #1 — GET /api/onboarding/instant-deal-hunt returns other organizations' leads by name

- **Where:** `server/routes-onboarding.ts:272-325 (query at :288-297); mounted at server/routes.ts:1537`
- **Defect:** The handler runs `db.select().from(leads).where(and(eq(leads.state, state), or(isNull(leads.county), eq(leads.county, county)))).orderBy(desc(leads.score)).limit(10)` with NO `organizationId` predicate, then renders the rows to the caller as "real motivated seller opportunities" including `ownerName`, `acreage`, `assessedValue`, motivation score/grade, `topSignal`, and derived offer/resale/profit figures, plus `totalScanned` as a live count. `leads.organizationId` is `NOT NULL`. Any authenticated user of any org can call this with an arbitrary state+county and read the highest-scored leads belonging to every other tenant operating in that market — which, in a land-investing product, is precisely the competitor next door. This is the exact shape CLAUDE.md records as the 2026-08-18 `generateDealFeed` cross-tenant read ("org-scoped six other ways… `await db.select().from(properties)` with no org predicate"), reintroduced in a file the tenancy lint never opens. The irony is load-bearing: the comment at :328-334 congratulates the endpoint for replacing fabricated opportunities with "real" ones — "real" here means another tenant's book.
- **Fix:** Add `eq(leads.organizationId, getOrganizationId(req))` to the `and(...)` at :291. Then close the class: the endpoint is a first-run "see value in 2 minutes" surface, so if the intent was cross-org market breadth, it must return AGGREGATES (counts, medians, distributions) computed over all orgs — never rows, never `ownerName`. Add the org predicate and a regression test that seeds two orgs and asserts org B's lead never appears in org A's response body.
- **Comparable:** Every mature multi-tenant backend makes this impossible by construction rather than by review: Supabase/Postgres RLS with `organization_id = current_setting('app.org_id')` forced at the session level, or Nile/Neon per-tenant schemas. AcreOS already has `forOrg()` in server/utils/orgScopedDb.ts — the pattern exists; adoption is the gap.
- **Evidence:** Read server/routes-onboarding.ts:272-345 in full and the mount at server/routes.ts:1537. Confirmed `leads` carries `organization_id NOT NULL` (shared/schema.ts). Found by scanning the population the org-scope lint does not read (server/routes-*.ts, server/ai/**, server/jobs/**) for `.from(<org-scoped table>)` chains with no org term — 278 candidate chains, of which this one is a confirmed live customer-facing leak.

#### [critical/S] MOST LIKELY 90-DAY INCIDENT #2 — every rate limiter in server/index.ts keys on the Cloudflare edge IP, so 300 req/min is shared by ALL customers behind one POP and bulk export is capped at 5/day globally

- **Where:** `server/index.ts:418-424 (aiLimiter), :460-471 (exportLimiter), :488-497 (apiLimiter), :510-521 (mcpLimiter); contrast :286 and :334 (authLimiter/authAttemptLimiter, done correctly)`
- **Defect:** Two compounding bugs. (a) All four limiters are registered at index.ts:427/472/500/523, but `clerkMiddleware` and `getOrCreateOrg` are only mounted inside `registerRoutes`, awaited at index.ts:714. So `getClerkAuth(req)?.userId` and `req.organization?.id` are ALWAYS undefined when these keyGenerators run — the userId/org lanes are dead and every request falls to the last resort. (b) That last resort is bare `req.ip`. server/utils/clientIp.ts:9-18 documents, in the repo's own words, that with `app.set("trust proxy", 1)` behind Cloudflare→Fly, `req.ip` resolves to the CLOUDFLARE EDGE IP: "Any limiter keying its IP component on `req.ip` was bucketing the whole internet into a few hundred CF edge addresses." That fix was adopted in `authLimiter` and `authAttemptLimiter` via `getClientIp()` and never applied to these four. Net effect in production: `apiLimiter` (300/min) is a shared bucket for every AcreOS user routed through the same CF POP — the SPA fires ~10 XHRs per navigation, so roughly four concurrently-active customers trip it and everyone in that POP gets 429s; and `exportLimiter` (5 per 24h) means the first five bulk exports from ANY customer lock out every other customer in that POP for a day, while its comment claims a "per-org daily cap."
- **Fix:** Move the four `app.use(...)` limiter mounts from index.ts to inside `registerRoutes`, AFTER the clerkMiddleware wrapper (server/routes.ts:745) and — for `exportLimiter` — after `getOrCreateOrg`; and replace every bare `req.ip` fallback with `ipKeyGenerator(getClientIp(req))`, matching authLimiter at index.ts:296. Then make the class un-reintroducible: add a test that boots the app, records the middleware stack order, and asserts no limiter whose keyGenerator references `getClerkAuth`/`req.organization` is registered before the Clerk layer — plus a lint forbidding bare `req.ip` inside a `keyGenerator`, since `getClientIp` exists precisely to be the only reader of it.
- **Comparable:** Cloudflare's own Rate Limiting and Fastly both key on the true client identity via `CF-Connecting-IP`/`Fastly-Client-IP`; Stripe and GitHub key their API budgets on the authenticated principal (key/user), never the network path. The repo already knows this — the fix is written down in clientIp.ts and applied to two of six limiters.
- **Evidence:** Read server/index.ts:418-523 and :714 (registration order), server/utils/clientIp.ts:1-50 (the documented CF-edge-IP hop analysis and the correct helper), server/types/request.ts:83-85 (`getClerkAuth` is a bare `req.auth` read), server/routes.ts:745 (clerkMiddleware mount) and :1537-1595 (getOrCreateOrg mounts). The correctly-keyed sibling family in server/middleware/rateLimit.ts:110/250/312 uses `getClientIp` throughout and is mounted after Clerk — but at 1000/min it is never the binding constraint, so the broken 300/min layer wins.

#### [critical/M] MOST LIKELY 90-DAY INCIDENT #3 — Reg-Z §1026.41 borrower statements are generated monthly from payment_applications, a table whose only writer lives in a module nothing imports

- **Where:** `server/services/paymentApplication/index.ts:318-408 (sole writer, :346); consumed by server/services/periodicStatements/index.ts:736-800 and server/services/lateFees/index.ts:199-212; fired by server/jobs/runScheduledJobs.ts:2248-2280; read by borrowers at server/routes-borrower.ts:1965-1990`
- **Defect:** `db.insert(paymentApplications)` at paymentApplication/index.ts:346 is the ONLY write to `payment_applications` anywhere in server/. Nothing imports that module — the repo's own reachability gate reports `server/services/paymentApplication/index.ts — nothing in production imports this file at all` (MODULE ORPHAN). Meanwhile the §1026.41 job at runScheduledJobs.ts:2248 runs on the 1st of every month for every active org, and periodicStatements/index.ts:738-795 builds `pastPaymentBreakdown` (principal/interest/escrow/fees/unapplied/totalReceived), the `transactions` array, and the YTD totals by summing that table. With no rows, every consumer-borrower periodic statement asserts $0 applied to principal, $0 to interest, $0 to escrow and an empty transaction list for a cycle in which the borrower paid — and the borrower reads it at `GET /api/borrower/periodic-statements`. `lateFees.creditedToCycle` (lateFees/index.ts:199) sums the same empty table, so a paying borrower reads as $0 credited to the cycle. A statement that states $0 for a real payment is not a missing feature; it is a false statement of account under Regulation Z, and it is exactly the "fabrication is never acceptable" line.
- **Fix:** Two separate fixes. (1) Wire it: call `applyPayment` from the real payment-posting paths (server/services/rental/paymentPosting.ts, server/services/achAutopay.ts settlement, and the manual note-payment routes in server/routes-notes.ts) so `payment_applications` is populated, then backfill from `note_payments`/`payments` for cycles already stated. (2) Fix the gate that let this look green: `tablesNoWriter` in scripts/lint-reachability.mjs counts a `db.insert()` that sits inside a module orphan as a writer. Make the writer-detection predicate require the writing module to be reachable, and add a rule that a table with zero REACHABLE writers and ≥1 reader on a delivered surface is a hard failure, not a baseline entry. Until it is wired, `generateStatementsForCycle` must REFUSE (Errors.notImplemented / skip-ledger row) rather than emit a $0 breakdown.
- **Comparable:** Servicing platforms that pass a CFPB exam (Sagent, MSP) never derive a statement line from a table without an ingestion contract test that asserts a posted payment appears on the next statement. The generic control is a golden-path integration test: post a payment → generate the cycle → assert the statement's principal/interest sum equals the payment. That test does not exist here.
- **Evidence:** Grepped every write to `paymentApplications` across server/ — one hit, at paymentApplication/index.ts:346. Grepped every import of `services/paymentApplication` — zero. Ran `node scripts/lint-reachability.mjs --report`, which independently reports the file as a MODULE ORPHAN ("nothing in production imports this file at all"). Read the statement generator's breakdown loop (periodicStatements/index.ts:750-785) and the monthly job body (runScheduledJobs.ts:2248-2280) to confirm the job is live and unconditional for active orgs.

#### [high/M] The org-scope tenancy lint proves its property over two directories and cannot see Drizzle's relational query API at all

- **Where:** `scripts/check-org-scoped-fetch.mjs:964-1000 (findScannedFiles), :1198 (LONE_ID_WHERE), :1258-1263 (Rule 3's `.from(` chain walker)`
- **Defect:** Two independent blind spots, both invisible in a green result. POPULATION: `findScannedFiles()` walks exactly `server/storage.ts`, `server/storage/*.ts` and `server/services/**`. It never opens `server/routes-*.ts` (≈280 files), `server/ai/**` (including `executeSupportTool`'s 76-case dispatch), `server/jobs/**`, `server/agents/**`, `server/api-v1/**` or `server/routes/**` — the files where request handlers actually live. Scanning that unread population for `.from(<org-scoped table>)` chains with no org term yields 278 candidates; one of them (finding #1) is a confirmed live cross-tenant leak. PREDICATE: Rule 2's regex is `/where\(\s*eq\(\s*(\w+)\.id/` and Rule 3 slices chains beginning at `.from(`. Drizzle's relational API writes neither — it is `db.query.leads.findFirst({ where: eq(leads.id, x) })`. So all 281 `db.query.*` call sites in server/ are outside BOTH rules' reach; 50 of them read an org-scoped table with no org term, including `server/services/landCredit.ts:1334/1354`, reached by `GET /api/land-credit/backtest/:propertyId` (routes-land-credit.ts:140-146) whose service method `backtestScore(propertyId)` takes no organizationId at all and resolves `landCreditScores` and `properties` by bare id.
- **Fix:** Put the enumeration in the test, per CLAUDE.md's third law: change `findScannedFiles()` to walk all of `server/**` with an explicit, named exclusion list, and add a per-directory vacuity assertion so a directory silently dropping out of the walk fails rather than reading as clean. Add a fourth extractor for the relational shape (`db.query.<table>.find(First|Many)({ where: … })`) that applies Rules 2 and 3 to the object-literal `where` value. Then mutation-test both: rename a real handler's org predicate away in `server/routes-leads.ts` and in one `db.query` call and confirm the lint goes red. Baseline the resulting backlog once; ratchet down.
- **Comparable:** This is the same discipline the repo already applies elsewhere and applies well — the `minima`/vacuity floors in scripts/ratchets/*.json ("a scan that stops seeing files finds zero and reports it as progress") are better than what most teams ship. The gap is that check-org-scoped-fetch has floors on its own scan but no floor asserting the scan covers the directories where routes live.
- **Evidence:** Read scripts/check-org-scoped-fetch.mjs end to end — the header at :66 states the population, `findScannedFiles` at :964-1000 implements it, `LONE_ID_WHERE` at :1198 and the Rule 3 chain slicer at :1258/:1356 both key on `.where(`/`.from(`. Wrote a scanner over the unread population using the same org-scoped-table extraction the lint uses (extended to all of shared/schema/*.ts, which raises the org-scoped table count from 230 to 364): 278 `.from(` hits and 7 relational hits outside the lint's reach. Verified `landCredit.backtestScore` by reading routes-land-credit.ts:140-146 and landCredit.ts:1327-1360.

#### [high/M] GET /api/today loads up to 20,000 wide rows to emit at most seven queue items, and its 5,000-row truncation drops exactly the stale leads the queue exists to find

- **Where:** `server/routes-today.ts:1085-1105 (the 9-wide Promise.all) and :744-796 (gatherAiQueue); server/storage/leadRepo.ts:17-30, dealRepo.ts:16-24, propertyRepo.ts:17-25, noteRepo.ts:63-71`
- **Defect:** The app's primary door issues `storage.getLeads/getDeals/getProperties/getNotes(orgId)` concurrently — each a `SELECT *` capped at `LIST_READ_CAP = 5000` (storage/listCap.ts:16) — then filters in JavaScript. `gatherAiQueue` (:744-756) uses the full lead array only to `.slice(0, 3)` after sorting by days-since-contact; `pendingDeals` takes `.slice(0, 2)`; `pendingProperties` `.slice(0, 2)`. Up to 20,000 wide rows cross the wire and land in a 1 GB Fly machine's heap (fly.toml:89-91) to produce seven items. Worse, it is also a CORRECTNESS defect: every capped read is `ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 5000`, so for any org past the cap the rows dropped are the OLDEST leads — which is precisely the population with the largest days-since-contact that `staleLeads` sorts for. In a large org the "most overdue follow-up" the Today queue surfaces is wrong by construction, and the only signal is a `logger.warn` nobody sees.
- **Fix:** Replace the four whole-table reads with purpose-built, indexed, LIMITed queries: `SELECT id, first_name, last_name, last_contacted_at FROM leads WHERE organization_id = $1 AND status NOT IN ('closed','dead') AND do_not_contact = false ORDER BY last_contacted_at ASC NULLS FIRST LIMIT 3`, and the equivalents for deals-by-status and properties-by-updated_at. Add the covering indexes (`leads (organization_id, last_contacted_at)`, `deals (organization_id, status)`). Where a whole-set read genuinely is needed, select only the columns used rather than `SELECT *`. Keep `capListRead` — it is the right guardrail — but no hot path should be near it.
- **Comparable:** Linear's inbox, Superhuman's split inbox and GitHub's notification feed all compute the ranked slice in the database with a covering index and a LIMIT; none pulls the workspace into app memory to choose three rows. The generic rule an elite team enforces: the number of rows a request loads should be proportional to what it renders, not to the size of the tenant.
- **Evidence:** Read server/routes-today.ts:1085-1160 and :734-800, the four repo methods, and server/storage/listCap.ts:16-32. Confirmed the mount at server/routes.ts:1444 and that `GET /` (routes-today.ts:1220) is the handler behind the Today door.

#### [high/S] GET /api/today performs writes inside a GET, with a read-then-insert race and no unique constraint, so duplicate alerts accumulate forever

- **Where:** `server/routes-today.ts:1226-1229; server/services/portfolioHealth.ts:33-44 (read) and :156-158 (insert)`
- **Defect:** The Today handler awaits `runPortfolioHealthJob(orgId)` on every render. That function SELECTs the org's undismissed `system_alerts` types into a `Set` (:34-44), builds `alertsToInsert`, then bulk-INSERTs (:157). There is no unique constraint on `system_alerts` (grepped migrations/ and scripts/migrate.mjs — none) and no transaction, so it is a plain TOCTOU: two concurrent Today renders (two tabs, react-query refetch-on-focus overlapping the initial load, or two teammates at 9am) both read the same empty set and both insert. Nothing dedupes afterwards, so an org steadily accumulates N copies of "3 Properties Need AVM Refresh" in both the Today queue and `/api/alerts/active`. Independently: a GET that mutates breaks HTTP semantics — any prefetch, retry, or double-render writes rows — and it puts a batch job's latency on the critical path of the app's first screen.
- **Fix:** Move the refresh out of the request: it already runs as a scheduled job, so the Today handler should only READ `system_alerts`. If a freshness guarantee is needed, gate it behind a per-org `last_refreshed_at` and fire it without awaiting. Independently, add `UNIQUE (organization_id, type) WHERE status NOT IN ('dismissed','resolved')` as a partial unique index and change the insert to `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` — the dedupe belongs in the database, not in a Set read a moment earlier. Add `system_alerts (organization_id, status)` while you are there (see the missing-index finding).
- **Comparable:** The repo already gets this right in two places — Stripe webhook claiming uses `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING` (webhookHandlers.ts:158-161) and `hydrateUser` uses the same pattern for the users row (clerkAuth.ts:246-256), with a comment explaining the exact race. The pattern is house style; it just was not applied here.
- **Evidence:** Read server/routes-today.ts:1220-1240 and server/services/portfolioHealth.ts:28-165. Grepped migrations/*.sql and scripts/migrate.mjs for any UNIQUE on system_alerts — zero hits; the same grep confirms system_alerts has no index on organization_id either.

#### [high/S] organizations.owner_id is unindexed and resolved on every authenticated request — twice, because /api mounts the auth chain a second time as a catch-all

- **Where:** `server/storage/orgRepo.ts:24-27 and :29-32; shared/schema.ts:62-67; server/routes.ts:1595; server/middleware/getOrCreateOrg.ts:60-135; server/auth/clerkAuth.ts:218-223`
- **Defect:** Two problems that multiply. (a) `getOrganizationByOwner` runs `SELECT * FROM organizations WHERE owner_id = $1` and `organizations` has exactly one non-PK index in the entire DDL (`idx_organizations_pause_resume` on subscription_paused/pause_ends_at, scripts/migrate.mjs:226) plus the unique on `slug` — there is NO index on `owner_id`. This is a sequential scan on the auth hot path of essentially every request. `getOrganizationByStripeCustomerId` (:29) is the same shape and seq-scans on every Stripe webhook. Because the app creates a shadow org per new user, this table grows with SIGNUPS, not with paying customers. (b) `app.use('/api', isAuthenticated, getOrCreateOrg, epicServicesRouter)` at routes.ts:1595 runs its middleware for EVERY `/api/*` request, and Express continues to later layers when the router does not match — so every route registered after line 1595 (roughly half the API surface: /api/tax-delinquent:2263, /api/kpis:2265, /api/accounting:2272, /api/deal-rooms:2311, …) pays `hydrateUser` (1 SELECT on users) + `getOrCreateOrg` (1-3 SELECTs) TWICE per request. With a 5-connection pool per process (db.ts:78), 12 XHRs per page nav is 48-96 auth round-trips.
- **Fix:** (1) `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY organizations_owner_id_idx ON organizations (owner_id)` and `organizations_stripe_customer_id_idx ON organizations (stripe_customer_id)`; add both to shared/schema.ts's index callback so the schema and the DB agree. (2) Make `epicServicesRouter` mount on its real path prefixes instead of the bare `/api` catch-all, or make `getOrCreateOrg` idempotent (`if (req.organizationId) return next()`) — a one-line guard that halves the duplicated cost immediately. (3) Strategically: resolve the tenant from the session rather than the database — put the active org id in a Clerk session claim, verified once at `switch-organization`, so the steady-state request does zero org queries.
- **Comparable:** Clerk (already the identity provider here), WorkOS Organizations and Auth0 Organizations all put `org_id` and `org_role` in the session JWT precisely so tenant resolution costs zero database round-trips. AcreOS pays 2-6 queries per request for something the token it already verifies could carry.
- **Evidence:** Read server/storage/orgRepo.ts:24-32 and shared/schema.ts:62-90 (no index callback on the organizations pgTable). Grepped all of migrations/*.sql and scripts/migrate.mjs for `CREATE INDEX … ON "organizations"` — one hit, the pause/resume composite. Read server/routes.ts:1570-1600 and 2260-2315 to confirm the catch-all precedes half the mounts; read server/auth/clerkAuth.ts:218-223 and server/middleware/getOrCreateOrg.ts:83-135 to count the per-request queries.

#### [high/M] withJobLock has no lease renewal and every TTL is deliberately set BELOW the job's own interval, so a slow run loses its lock mid-flight

- **Where:** `server/utils/jobRuntime.ts:42-97; server/storage/platformOpsRepo.ts:214-255; TTL constants at server/jobs/runScheduledJobs.ts:78/119/314/344 ("Lock TTL slightly less than interval"), :370, server/jobs/leadCampaignJobs.ts:93/167, server/jobs/achAutopayRun.ts:85`
- **Defect:** `withJobLock` acquires a TTL'd row and never touches it again until the `finally` release. The house convention — repeated verbatim at seven call sites — is "Lock TTL slightly less than interval", which guarantees the lock expires BEFORE the next tick even when the run is still in flight. `pax_scheduler` holds 55s against a 60s interval; `atlas_pending_confirmation_nudge` 55s; `lead_nurturing` 14min against 15; `ach_autopay_cycle` 55min against hourly. Any run that outlives its TTL — one slow LLM call, one provider timeout, one large org — releases the lock to the next machine while still executing, and both machines then run the same job concurrently. The acquire is correctly atomic and `releaseJobLock` is correctly owner-scoped (a losing process cannot steal the release), so the failure is silent: no error, no `skipped_lock` row, just two concurrent runs. The blast radius is per-job and is entirely uncontrolled — `pax_nudges` and the atlas nudger send customer-facing messages, `digest` sends mail.
- **Fix:** Two changes. (1) Invert the TTL convention: a lock TTL must exceed the job's WORST-CASE runtime, not sit under its interval — a lock that outlives a crashed process is recovered by the expiry, which is what expiry is for. (2) Add a heartbeat: while `fn()` runs, extend `expires_at` on an interval of TTL/3 (`UPDATE job_locks SET expires_at = now() + ttl WHERE job_name = $1 AND locked_by = $2`) and abort the run if the extend affects zero rows, meaning the lock was lost. Simpler alternative with no TTL at all: `pg_try_advisory_lock(hashtext(job_name))` held for the connection's lifetime. Then falsify it: write a test that makes `fn()` sleep past the TTL and asserts a second `withJobLock` call for the same job returns null.
- **Comparable:** Temporal, Sidekiq Enterprise's unique jobs and Quartz all renew or fence: the lock is either heartbeated or carries a monotonically increasing fencing token that the write path checks. Postgres advisory locks — a one-line change here — remove the TTL question entirely by tying the lock to the session. Credit where due: ACH submission does NOT rely on the lock for money safety (achAutopayRun.ts:11-16 documents a unique claim per (note, period)), which is the correct layering and should be the template for the other lanes.
- **Evidence:** Read server/utils/jobRuntime.ts:42-97 (no renewal between acquire at :47 and release at :95) and server/storage/platformOpsRepo.ts:214-262 (atomic conditional UPDATE on `expires_at < now() OR locked_by = me`, then owner-scoped DELETE). Enumerated all 185 `withJobLock(` call sites and read the TTL constants; the "slightly less than interval" comment appears at seven of them.

#### [high/S] The approval kernel strands rows in 'approved' forever — invisible to the queue, invisible to the badge, and never swept

- **Where:** `server/services/approvalKernel.ts:383-393 (the claim), :410-425 (release on failure), :231-237 (livePendingPredicate), :275-289 (sweepExpiredPendingActions)`
- **Defect:** `approvePendingAction` claims the row `pending → approved` (:383-393) and only THEN executes the tool (:410). If the process dies in that window — a Fly machine replaced mid-deploy, an OOM, or the `uncaughtException` handler at index.ts:65-75 calling `process.exit(1)` — the row stays `approved` forever. `livePendingPredicate` (:232-236) filters on `status = 'pending'`, so both `listPendingActions` and `countPendingActions` skip it; `sweepExpiredPendingActions` (:281) also only touches `status = 'pending'`. Nothing anywhere reads or repairs an `approved` row. Observable behaviour for a paying customer: they tap Approve, the card disappears from the queue, the email is never sent, the badge count is correct, and there is no record of the failure. A second, subtler variant: when `execute` returns `success:false` the row is released to `pending` (:414-424) with no provider-side idempotency key — so a send that actually succeeded but whose response was lost (SES accepted, socket timed out) is re-offered and, on the second tap, sent twice.
- **Fix:** Add `claimed_at` alongside the status transition and extend `sweepExpiredPendingActions` to release `approved` rows with `claimed_at < now() - interval '5 minutes'` and no `executed_at` back to `pending` (or to a new `execution_unknown` state that the UI surfaces honestly — 'we could not confirm this send'). Separately, derive a provider idempotency key from the row's existing `contentHash` and pass it to the send path so a retry after a lost response is a no-op at the provider rather than a second message. Both are small; the kernel's core design — frozen args, sha256 re-verified at approval, guarded single-winner claim, append-only pax_sends — is right and should not be touched.
- **Comparable:** Stripe's `Idempotency-Key` is the canonical answer to the lost-response half; the stranded-claim half is the standard outbox/claim-check pattern (Debezium outbox, AWS SQS visibility timeout) where a claim without a completion is automatically re-queued after a visibility window rather than lost.
- **Evidence:** Read server/services/approvalKernel.ts:336-470 (the full approve path) and :231-289 (the predicate and the sweeper). Grepped for any other reader or writer of `status = 'approved'` in the file and repo — none outside this function.

#### [high/S] applyPayment mutates a borrower suspense balance with an unguarded read-modify-write — a lost update on regulated money

- **Where:** `server/services/paymentApplication/index.ts:322-330 (the read), :388-400 (the JSONB read-modify-write)`
- **Defect:** `applyPayment` (1) SELECTs the loan's `suspense_balances` row with no `FOR UPDATE`, (2) INSERTs `payment_applications` with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` on `payment_id` — correctly idempotent per payment — then (3) UPDATEs `suspense_balances` to an ABSOLUTE `balanceCents` computed from the value read in step 1, and overwrites `ledgerEntries` with `[...existingSuspense.ledgerEntries, entry]`. There is no `db.transaction` and no row lock. Two different payments on the same loan applied concurrently (a borrower payment posting while the ACH reconcile sweep settles another, or the same job running twice per the lock finding above) both pass the per-payment unique index and both compute from the same stale balance: the second UPDATE silently discards the first payment's suspense delta AND drops its ledger entry from the JSONB audit trail. This is money in a Reg-Z §1026.36(c) suspense account, and the lost row is the one an examiner would ask about.
- **Fix:** Wrap steps 1-3 in `db.transaction` and take `SELECT … FROM suspense_balances WHERE loan_id = $1 FOR UPDATE` — the repo already uses `.for("update")` correctly at server/routes-borrower.ts:1138, so the pattern is house style. Better still, make the write relative and atomic in one statement: `UPDATE suspense_balances SET balance_cents = balance_cents + $delta, ledger_entries = ledger_entries || $entry::jsonb WHERE loan_id = $1`, which removes the read-modify-write entirely.
- **Comparable:** Every ledger implementation that survives audit does this in one statement or under a row lock: Stripe's balance transactions, Modern Treasury's ledger API, and the classic Rails `with_lock` / `update_counters` pair. A JSONB array rebuilt in application memory is the append-only audit trail most likely to lose a row.
- **Evidence:** Read server/services/paymentApplication/index.ts:318-408 in full; confirmed no `db.transaction`, no `.for("update")`, and that `newSuspenseBalanceCents` is computed by `decidePaymentApplication` from the value read at :329. Scanned server/** for multi-write functions touching financial tables without a transaction — this is the only hit, which says the rest of the money layer is disciplined. NOTE: this is LATENT, not live — the module has no production importer today (see the periodic-statements finding), so no borrower has been harmed yet. It is the code that will be wired.

#### [high/S] SNS replay protection is a per-process in-memory Map — it does not survive a second machine or a restart

- **Where:** `server/middleware/snsVerification.ts:26-49; consumers at server/routes-ses-events.ts:266, server/routes-inbound-email.ts:56, server/middleware/inboundEmailSignature.ts:132/208`
- **Defect:** `isReplay()` records seen message ids in a module-scoped `Map` with a 24h TTL. fly.toml runs an autoscaled `app` group plus a separate `worker` process, so in production there is more than one Node process and no shared state between them: a message id seen on machine A is unknown to machine B, and every deploy or restart wipes the whole window. The four call sites are the SES bounce/complaint feed, the inbound-email ingest, and the HMAC inbound lane — i.e. the paths that demote sending identities and create customer conversations. SNS is at-least-once by design, so this is not only an attacker story: normal AWS redelivery routed to a different machine double-counts a bounce, and repeated double-counting drives an org's own connected sending identity into suppression. An attacker who captures one validly-signed SNS bounce can replay it freely to deny a competitor's outbound mail.
- **Fix:** Move the replay set to the shared store the repo already uses for exactly this reason: a Redis `SET NX EX 86400` on `sns:<MessageId>` (the codebase already has `createLimiterStore` in server/middleware/limiterRedisStore.ts built to solve the identical multi-machine problem for rate limits), or a `sns_processed_events` table with a unique index and `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING`, mirroring `stripeProcessedEvents` in webhookHandlers.ts:156-169 — which is the same problem solved correctly one file away.
- **Comparable:** The repo's own rate-limiter comment states the principle verbatim: "undefined store = stock per-instance MemoryStore, which on N machines silently grants N× the budget" (server/index.ts:262-265). That reasoning was applied to the limiters and never to the replay cache. AWS's own guidance for SNS/SQS consumers is a shared idempotency store (DynamoDB/Redis), never process memory.
- **Evidence:** Read server/middleware/snsVerification.ts:26-52 (module-scoped Map, `pruneReplayCache` bounded at 10,000 entries, `_resetReplayCache` test hook) and the four `isReplay` call sites. Read fly.toml:47-107 (an `app` group with autoscaling from min_machines_running = 1, plus a separate worker VM). Read webhookHandlers.ts:156-169 for the correct in-repo pattern.

#### [high/M] requestTimeout returns 504 but never cancels the work behind it, so a timed-out mutation completes anyway and the retry runs it twice

- **Where:** `server/middleware/security.ts:196-220; registered globally at server/index.ts:206 and AGAIN for /api at server/routes.ts:1044-1051`
- **Defect:** After the timer fires, the middleware writes a 504 and returns — the handler keeps running to completion: its DB writes commit, its provider calls fire, its emails send. There is no `AbortController`, no `AbortSignal` threaded into pg or into the HTTP clients, and no request-scoped cancellation of any kind. The client (client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:20, `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS = 30_000`) aborts at the same 30s and react-query surfaces a failure the user retries — so any slow POST that crosses 30s is executed twice with no idempotency key anywhere on the mutating surface. Secondary: the middleware is registered twice for every `/api` request (index.ts:206 globally, routes.ts:1044 again for /api), so each API request arms two independent timers. And the 504 body it writes (`{error: "Gateway Timeout", message, statusCode}`) bypasses `Errors.gatewayTimeout`, which exists at server/utils/errors.ts:246 for exactly this.
- **Fix:** Create an `AbortController` per request, abort it on timeout and on `res.on('close')`, and thread the signal into every outbound `fetch` and into the pg query path (`pool.query` accepts no signal, so pair it with a per-statement `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` inside the request's transaction). Independently — and this is the part that stops duplicate writes regardless of cancellation — accept a client-supplied `Idempotency-Key` header on POST/PUT/PATCH, store `(org_id, key) → response` and replay the stored response on retry. Remove one of the two registrations and route the 504 through `Errors.gatewayTimeout`.
- **Comparable:** Stripe's `Idempotency-Key` (24h replay of the original response) is the industry standard for exactly this failure and is what an elite team would require before shipping any money- or message-sending POST. Node's `undici`/`fetch` `AbortSignal.timeout()` and Go's `context.Context` are the cancellation half; a 504 without cancellation is a lie about what the server did.
- **Evidence:** Read server/middleware/security.ts:196-220 (timer writes a response, nothing else), server/index.ts:206 and server/routes.ts:1044-1051 (two registrations), client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:20 (matching 30s client abort). Grepped server/ for `AbortController` on the request path — no request-scoped cancellation exists.

#### [medium/S] Stripe webhook idempotency fails OPEN on a database error and is not crash-safe once claimed

- **Where:** `server/webhookHandlers.ts:156-169 (claimEvent), :171-210 (processWebhook), :212-228 (releaseClaim)`
- **Defect:** Two gaps in an otherwise well-built handler. (a) `claimEvent` catches ANY error from the claim INSERT and returns `true` — "Table may not exist yet during migration — allow processing" (:164-168). Migrations run exclusively as Fly's `release_command` (documented at server/index.ts:527-534), so the table always exists before boot; what the catch actually covers now is transient DB pressure or pgBouncer pool exhaustion, and in exactly that condition it disables idempotency and lets a concurrent redelivery double-process a payment event. An idempotency guard that fails open is not a guard. (b) The claim is written before dispatch and only released on a thrown error (:206-208). If the process dies mid-dispatch — OOM, machine replacement, or the `uncaughtException` handler's `process.exit(1)` — the claim row survives, and Stripe's retry is skipped as a duplicate. That is precisely the charged-but-not-provisioned scenario the comment at :193-201 says was fixed; the throw path was fixed, the crash path was not.
- **Fix:** (a) Delete the fail-open catch: a failed claim must return `false` and let the route return non-2xx so Stripe retries — the on-call page at index.ts:239-249 already exists for this. (b) Make the claim two-phase: add `completed_at` to `stripe_processed_events`, stamp it after successful dispatch, and treat a claim older than N minutes with a NULL `completed_at` as releasable so a crashed run is redelivered rather than swallowed.
- **Comparable:** Stripe's own integration guide recommends recording the event id and marking completion, not just claiming it. Shopify and Segment webhook consumers use the same claim/complete pair. Notably, the Lob handler one directory over (server/routes/lob-webhooks.ts:227-245) is idempotent by CONSTRUCTION — monotone status rank plus `COALESCE` write-once timestamps — which needs no claim table at all and is the better pattern where the event is a state transition.
- **Evidence:** Read server/webhookHandlers.ts:137-230 in full, including the fail-open catch at :164-168 and the release-on-throw at :206-208. Read server/index.ts:527-534 (migrations are a release_command, so the stated justification for the catch is stale) and :65-75 (the uncaughtException handler that exits the process).

#### [medium/L] 109 org-scoped tables have no index on organization_id anywhere, and the lint that claims to guard this reads the Drizzle schema while the real DDL lives in migrations/

- **Where:** `scripts/check-org-leading-index.mjs:20-30 (it parses shared/schema*.ts) vs migrations/*.sql + scripts/migrate.mjs (where CREATE INDEX actually lives); example tables: shared/schema.ts:3218 (offers), :7409 (generated_documents), signatures, system_alerts, agent_events, scheduled_tasks, buyer_reservations, trust_ledger, autopay_enrollments`
- **Defect:** Extracting every org-scoped pgTable from shared/schema.ts + shared/schema-*.ts + shared/schema/*.ts (364 tables) and cross-referencing every `CREATE INDEX` in migrations/*.sql and scripts/migrate.mjs (836 statements) leaves 109 tables with NO index mentioning `organization_id` — including tables on live customer paths (`offers`, `generated_documents`, `signatures`, `system_alerts`, `escrow_checklists`, `closing_packets`, `autopay_enrollments`, `trust_ledger`). Two consequences. Read: every per-org query on those tables is a sequential scan, degrading linearly with total platform rows rather than with tenant size. Write/delete: Postgres does NOT auto-index a foreign-key column, and each of these declares `organization_id ... references(() => organizations.id)`, so deleting an organization (GDPR/DSAR erasure, org merge) forces a full scan of every one of the 109 child tables while holding locks. Separately, the gate meant to prevent this measures the wrong artifact: `check-org-leading-index.mjs` parses index declarations out of shared/schema*.ts, but the indexes that exist in production were written as raw SQL in migrations — so its 148-entry BASELINE_OFFENDERS list conflates "missing in production" with "declared in a different file," and neither number answers the operational question.
- **Fix:** (1) Generate the missing indexes from the cross-reference above and add them as one `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` migration, prioritising the tables the hot doors filter (`offers`, `system_alerts`, `generated_documents`, `signatures`, `scheduled_tasks`). (2) Fix the gate's population: have `check-org-leading-index.mjs` build its index inventory from the union of the Drizzle schema AND the migration DDL, so it reports what the DATABASE has rather than what one file declares — and add a vacuity floor on the DDL statement count, the same discipline the ratchet JSONs already apply to their file counts. (3) Best proof: run `pg_stat_user_tables` in production and fail the gate on any org-scoped table with `seq_scan` materially exceeding `idx_scan` — that measures the property instead of the declaration.
- **Comparable:** Postgres's own documentation is explicit that FK columns are not auto-indexed and that this is the standard cause of slow parent deletes; `pganalyze` and AWS Performance Insights both flag it as a first-class finding. Rails' `add_reference … index: true` and Prisma's `@@index` default to indexing the FK for this reason.
- **Evidence:** Wrote a cross-reference over shared/schema.ts + shared/schema-*.ts + shared/schema/*.ts (364 org-scoped tables by the same `organizationId: …("organization_id")` predicate the lint uses) against every CREATE INDEX in migrations/*.sql and scripts/migrate.mjs: 109 tables with zero organization_id coverage. Spot-verified by direct grep that `offers`, `signatures` and `generated_documents` have no index DDL at all, and that `organizations` itself has only `idx_organizations_pause_resume` (scripts/migrate.mjs:226). Read scripts/check-org-leading-index.mjs:20-75 to confirm its input is shared/schema*.ts only.

#### [medium/S] The last-resort Express error handler breaks the documented API error contract on exactly the failures nobody anticipated

- **Where:** `server/index.ts:745-757 (the generic handler, response at :753); contract defined at server/utils/errors.ts:8-24 and consumed at client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:25-35`
- **Defect:** CLAUDE.md mandates that all responses conform to `{ error, message, details?, statusCode }`, and server/utils/errors.ts implements that beautifully — thirteen typed helpers, `docsUrl` deep links, and `requestId` on 5xx so a user can hand support a correlation id. The terminal handler emits `res.status(status).json({ message })` — no `error` code, no `statusCode`, no `docsUrl`, no `requestId`. Since this repo runs Express 5, every unhandled async rejection in any of ~2,700 route handlers lands here, as do body-parser JSON syntax errors and anything thrown by middleware. So the ONE class of failure where a correlation id matters most — the unanticipated one — is the class that ships without it. The client degrades gracefully (queryClient.ts:229, `parsed?.message ?? text`) so nothing crashes; the user simply gets "Internal Server Error" with nothing to report.
- **Fix:** Replace the body with `Errors.internal(res, err)` (and keep the existing 423 LegalHold branch above it, which is correct). That single change gives every unhandled error the `INTERNAL_ERROR` code, the production-safe message, the KB link and the `requestId` the correlation middleware already stamps. Then falsify it: add a route that throws asynchronously and assert the response body has all four contract fields — that assertion is what stops the next terminal handler from drifting.
- **Comparable:** Stripe, Shopify and GitHub all return a stable machine-readable `type`/`code` plus a request id on every error including 500s — Shopify's `X-Request-Id` is the first thing their support asks for. AcreOS already built that; it just is not on the path that needs it most.
- **Evidence:** Read server/index.ts:713-760 (the handler chain: errorLoggingMiddleware → Sentry → this), server/utils/errors.ts:1-330 (the contract and the `resolveRequestId` helper), client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:25-35 and :228-231 (the client's declared `ApiErrorBody` and its fallback). Confirmed express ^5.2.1 in package.json:169, so async throws do reach this handler.

#### [medium/S] CSRF failures return a bare 403 with no machine-readable code and no client-side token refresh, so a save silently does nothing

- **Where:** `server/middleware/csrf.ts:139-145; client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:322-326 (readCsrfToken) and :246-249 (403 handling on queries)`
- **Defect:** On a token mismatch the middleware writes `res.status(403).json({ message: "CSRF token validation failed" })` — no `error` code, so no client can branch on it. The `csrf_token` cookie is a SESSION cookie with no maxAge (csrf.ts:104-107); any browser state that clears it (a privacy extension, ITP, a partially-cleared site data, a restored tab in a new session) leaves the SPA holding a stale mirrored value while the server has issued a new one. The mutation then 403s and the user sees a generic red toast reading "CSRF token validation failed" — technical, non-actionable, and with no retry. There is no interceptor that, on a CSRF 403, re-reads the cookie (or does a cheap GET to have one issued) and retries the mutation once. The result a customer experiences is "I clicked Save and it didn't save, and the error made no sense."
- **Fix:** Return `Errors.forbidden(res, "Your security token expired — refresh the page and try again.", { docsSlug: "csrf" })` with a distinguishable code (add `Errors.csrfFailed` if a distinct `error` string is wanted), and add one interceptor in queryClient.ts: on 403 with that code, issue a GET to refresh the cookie, re-read it, and retry the mutation exactly once. Consider also binding the token to the session (HMAC of the Clerk session id) rather than an unsigned double-submit, which closes the subdomain cookie-tossing weakness inherent to unsigned double-submit.
- **Comparable:** Rails + Turbo transparently refresh the CSRF token and retry; Django's CSRF failure view renders an explicit, actionable page; Stripe's dashboard silently re-issues. OWASP's CSRF cheat sheet recommends the signed (HMAC) double-submit over the unsigned form for exactly the cookie-tossing reason.
- **Evidence:** Read server/middleware/csrf.ts:1-148 (exempt list, cookie issuance at :99-110, the raw 403 at :143) and client/src/lib/queryClient.ts:190-232 (error parsing), :294-318 (mutation toast), :322-336 (token mirroring). The exempt-path list and its rationale comments are exemplary; the failure response is the gap.

#### [medium/S] /api/data-api is mounted after the /api isAuthenticated catch-all, so its "API key auth handled internally" comment is false

- **Where:** `server/routes.ts:2312 (`app.use('/api/data-api', dataApiRouter); // API key auth handled internally`) vs the catch-all at :1595`
- **Defect:** `app.use('/api', isAuthenticated, getOrCreateOrg, epicServicesRouter)` at :1595 runs `isAuthenticated` for every `/api/*` request, and `isAuthenticated` terminates with 401 when there is no Clerk session (server/auth/clerkAuth.ts:326-331 → hydrateUser's 401 at :215). An external integrator presenting an `ak_live_…` bearer key and no Clerk cookie therefore gets 401 at line 1595 and never reaches `dataApiRouter`.
